INTRODUCTION\\r\\nWater is commonly supplied to households through a Wa-\\r\\nter Distribution Network (WDN). Kingdom et al. (2006)\\r\\nfound that globally on average, around 35% of produced\\r\\nwater is lost during distribution, with strong societal and\\r\\neconomic implications. It is therefore fundamental to de-\\r\\nvelop efficient techniques for the prompt detection and\\r\\nisolation of leakages in WDNs. However, major obstacles\\r\\nare represented by the scarcity of measurements and the\\r\\nuncertainty in demand, making the leakage isolation prob-\\r\\nlem a very challenging one.\\r\\nMethods for detecting the presence of leaks in WDNs can\\r\\nbe classified as passive and active. Passive leak detec-\\r\\ntion systems are those that require maintenance person-\\r\\nnel to survey a particular site in search of leaks (Chan\\r\\net al. (2018)). These will thus not be helpful in reducing\\r\\nawareness times of hidden leakages with any significance,\\r\\nalthough they remain very valid for locating faults once\\r\\ndetected. Active systems are those which use continuous\\r\\nmonitoring of signals from the network to detect leakages\\r\\nin an automated manner. These may be further classified\\r\\ninto model-based and data-driven approaches as described\\r\\nby Chan et al. (2018). Model-based approaches for leak\\r\\ndetection, rely on the creation of hydraulic mathematical\\r\\nmodels that simulate the water distribution network, and\\r\\nare used for estimating expected conditions at a given\\r\\npoint in the network, e.g., Wu et al. (2018); Soldevila\\r\\net al. (2016). This model can be learned exploiting the\\r\\ntopological structure of the network, as done by Vrachimis\\r\\net al. (2021), where a node’s hydraulic state is determined\\r\\nby modelling the WDN as a graph with nodes representing\\r\\njunctions, water demand locations, reservoirs and tanks,\\r\\nand edges representing pipes. This method obtained strong\\r\\n⋆The work of G.Ö.Garðarsson has been supported by the Chevening\\r\\nScholarship, the UK government’s global scholarship programme,\\r\\nfunded by the Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Office\\r\\n(FCDO) and partner organisations\\r\\ndetection performance and good localisation for significant\\r\\nleaks, but it relies on historical demand data, which may\\r\\nnot be accessible everywhere, as some utilities bill cus-\\r\\ntomers from their property’s size, rather than by meter.\\r\\nOn the other hand, data-driven approaches learn to detect\\r\\nleaks given historical data from the WDN. WDN sen-\\r\\nsor measurements are commonly collected in supervisory\\r\\ncontrol and data acquisition (SCADA) systems and have\\r\\nhistorically been constrained to few, sparse measurements\\r\\npoints. These methods however require large amounts of\\r\\ndata Chan et al. (2018). Their development is further\\r\\nhampered by leak data being a minority in the data\\r\\ncollected Mounce et al. (2010), and measurement noise.\\r\\nRomero et al. (2020) introduces a data-driven method that\\r\\nexploits pressure measurements. The complete network\\r\\nstate estimation is achieved by linear interpolation by\\r\\nsolving an optimisation problem. However the method is,\\r\\ni) only applied to densely monitored regions of the WDN,\\r\\nii) topology-agnostic, which might result in limited predic-\\r\\ntion especially in sparsely monitored regions or in highly\\r\\ndata-hungry learning models. Thus, the importance of\\r\\ndeveloping methodologies that are topology-(graph-)aware\\r\\nis apparent.\\r\\nA model-transient-based approach that utilises deep-\\r\\nlearning for leak identification was proposed by Kang et al.\\r\\n(2018), where graph-based search was used for leak lo-\\r\\ncalisation. Specifically, the proposed method detects leaks\\r\\nas transient oscillations in the vibration signals, using a\\r\\nconvolutional neural network (CNN). After leak detection,\\r\\na localisation algorithm is proposed using the graph-based\\r\\nwavelet method introduced in Srirangarajan et al. (2013),\\r\\nshowing good performance. However, the latter has been\\r\\ncarried out with engineered tests at night, resulting in\\r\\nunrealistic test conditions Wu and Liu (2017). Despite this,\\r\\ntheir graph-based method was accurate in localisation.\\r\\nIn this paper, we propose a novel active method for\\r\\nleakage detection and isolation that combines data-driven\\r\\nKeywords: Fault detection and diagnosis, water distribution systems, geometric deep learning.\\r\\nAbstract: We propose the application of geometric deep learning techniques to the challenging\\r\\nleak detection and isolation problem in water distribution networks (WDNs). Specifically, we\\r\\ntrain two Chebyshev polynomial kernel Graph Convolutional Networks for the task of prediction,\\r\\nand reconstruction of nodal pressures in a WDN. Comparing the two network outputs (a\\r\\npredicted healthy model state with a reconstructed observation) a residual signal is obtained\\r\\nand analysed to detect leakages. By exploiting topological properties in the proposed approach,\\r\\nleakage isolation is also performed. We benchmark our method on the BattLeDIM 2020 dataset.\\r\\n∗EEE Department, University College London\\r\\ngardar.gardarsson.20@alumni.ucl.ac.uk, {f.boem, l.toni}@ucl.ac.uk\\r\\nGarðar Örn Garðarsson ∗Francesca Boem ∗Laura Toni ∗\\r\\nGraph-Based Learning for Leak Detection\\r\\nand Localisation in Water Distribution\\r\\nNetworks ⋆\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 2 ===\\r\\n662\\r\\nGarðar Örn Garðarsson et al. / IFAC PapersOnLine 55-6 (2022) 661–666\\r\\nFig. 1. Proposed architecture, two GNNs reconstruct and predict complete pressure scenes, ˆy(t)\\r\\nr\\r\\nand ˆy(t)\\r\\np , from a sparse\\r\\ngraph signal, x(t)\\r\\nG , and a set thereof, X(t−T :t−1)\\r\\nG\\r\\n= [x(t−T )\\r\\nG\\r\\n, x(t−T +1)\\r\\nG\\r\\n, ..., x(t−1)\\r\\nG\\r\\n], respectively. The difference between\\r\\nthe two, ˆr(t)\\r\\nV , represents a per-node prediction error, which is translated to a per-edge basis ˆr(t)\\r\\nE , such that fault\\r\\ndetection methods applied to the signal directly returns a leaky pipe candidate, in line with benchmark objectives.\\r\\ntechniques to learn a WDN model, with a model-based\\r\\napproach for detection. Similarly to Romero et al. (2020),\\r\\nwe aim to estimate the complete network state from\\r\\ndata, but we consider a topology-aware approach. Namely,\\r\\nwhere Romero et al. (2020) use linear-graph interpolation\\r\\nto obtain the complete network state based on the sparse\\r\\nsensor measurements, we adopt a Graph Neural Network\\r\\n(GNN) with a Chebyshev kernel to predict the complete\\r\\nstate of the network. In more detail, we propose a novel\\r\\nmethod based on GNNs, that exploit the topology of the\\r\\nWDN to reconstruct and predict nodal pressures where\\r\\nmeasurements are not available. The GNN regressors are\\r\\ntrained on a hydraulic simulation of the WDN in a healthy\\r\\nstate before being applied to sparse pressure measurement\\r\\ndata. From the reconstructed (observed) and predicted\\r\\n(expected) pressure states, a discrepancy is calculated, on\\r\\nwhich a statistical fault detection method is applied to\\r\\nlocate anomalies such as leaks.\\r\\nThis work thereby addresses the leakage localisation prob-\\r\\nlem with relatively few priors, and is not dependant on\\r\\ndemand or flow data. Beyond being topology-aware, our\\r\\nmethod differs from existing ones in the residual gener-\\r\\nation. While Romero et al. (2020) analyse least square\\r\\ndistances to the best line fitted through the point cloud\\r\\nformed by comparing the inferred network state with the\\r\\nnominal state, we convert nodal prediction errors to resid-\\r\\nual signals on the edges (corresponding to the pipes of\\r\\na WDN), and evaluate their moving averages against a\\r\\nstatistical threshold, to identify leak candidates. For the\\r\\ngraph-based inference, we build on contributions by Ha-\\r\\njgató et al. (2021) for nodal pressure reconstruction, by\\r\\n1) extending the learning objective for nodal pressure\\r\\nprediction; 2) analysing different edge-weight assignment\\r\\nof the graph, and 3) introducing a new graph constructions\\r\\nfrom the WDN based on self-loops to sensor nodes, which\\r\\nled to better detection.\\r\\nLooking at works that adopted GNNs, Hajgató et al.\\r\\n(2021) recently published their findings on their applica-\\r\\ntion for nodal pressure signal reconstruction. Their inputs\\r\\nare nodal observations, i.e., hydraulic states of the mon-\\r\\nitored nodes, and their outputs are the pressures of all\\r\\nnodes. Values proportional to the hydraulic loss in the\\r\\nnetwork’s pipes, and logarithmic variants thereof, were\\r\\nassigned to the adjacency matrix but not found to yield im-\\r\\nprovements over a binary one. Our paper exploits instead\\r\\nthe nodal reconstruction logic proposed in Hajgató et al.\\r\\n(2021) and extends it for the purpose of leakage detection\\r\\nand isolation. While GNNs have been applied to model\\r\\nWDNs in the work of Garzón et al. (2021), to the best of\\r\\nour knowledge this is the first work exploiting GNNs for\\r\\nleakage detection and localisation.\\r\\n2. PROPOSED METHODOLOGY\\r\\nWe now introduce our proposed solution to the problem\\r\\nof leakage detection and localisation, aimed at identifying\\r\\nleakages from a partly observed WDN. To address it, the\\r\\nhydraulic state of the WDN is estimated at every inter-\\r\\nsection by processing physical time-series signals from the\\r\\nnetwork in conjunction with topological information about\\r\\nthe piping system. As depicted in Fig. 1, we design two\\r\\ndeep learning models trained under non-leaky conditions,\\r\\ni) a reconstructor, that infers nodal pressure for the current\\r\\ntime step given sparse sensor measurements, and ii) a\\r\\npredictor, that predicts nodal pressure for the next time\\r\\nstep, given a window of previous measurements.\\r\\nWe denote the data observed at time t on node v of the\\r\\nWDN by x(t)\\r\\nv . The signal observed at time t at all nodes\\r\\nis denoted by x(t)\\r\\nG\\r\\n= [x(t)\\r\\n1 , x(t)\\r\\n2 , . . . , x(t)\\r\\nN ], with x(t)\\r\\nv\\r\\n=NaN\\r\\nif the data is not observed at that location at time t.\\r\\nWe assume a sparse signal such that the time-varying\\r\\ndata is observed only at a few locations, meaning that\\r\\nthe majority of x(t)\\r\\nv\\r\\nwill be NaN. Given x(t)\\r\\nG\\r\\nas input,\\r\\nthe reconstructor infers the pressure for all missing nodes;\\r\\nwhich we denote by ˆy(t)\\r\\nr . The predictor instead predicts\\r\\nthe complete nodal pressures given historical data ob-\\r\\nserved over a time-widow of T instants and denoted by\\r\\nX(t−T :t−1)\\r\\nG\\r\\n= [x(t−T )\\r\\nG\\r\\n, x(t−T +1)\\r\\nG\\r\\n, . . . x(t−1)\\r\\nG\\r\\n]. The output of\\r\\nthe predictor, namely ˆy(t)\\r\\np , is compared with ˆy(t)\\r\\nr\\r\\nto gen-\\r\\nerate a residual. The node residual is then converted into\\r\\nedge-residual and analysed to localise leakages. Following\\r\\nis a description of how we construct the graph from the\\r\\nWDN topology, and how we identify the problem of in-\\r\\nference as a graph signal processing (GSP) method. We\\r\\nthen describe, i) the architecture that we propose for the\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 3 ===\\r\\n\\r\\nGarðar Örn Garðarsson et al. / IFAC PapersOnLine 55-6 (2022) 661–666\\r\\n663\\r\\nFig. 2. Undirected graph construction from topological\\r\\ninformation about the WDN. Data collected over time\\r\\nat sensor location represents the signal on graph.\\r\\ntwo networks (reconstructor and predictor), and ii) the\\r\\nconversion and analysis of the residual.\\r\\n2.1 Topological Graph Construction\\r\\nWe start with modeling a WDN (topological objects of\\r\\njunctions connected by pipe segments) using a graph, as\\r\\nshown in Fig. 2.\\r\\nGiven an undirected graph G = (V, E), V is a set of nodes\\r\\nwhere each v ∈V denotes a node on the graph (junctions\\r\\nof the WDN) and we consider N nodes (i.e., |V| = N);\\r\\nE ⊆V × V is a set of edges with eij ∈E connecting node\\r\\ni and node j. It is worth noting that water in WDNs\\r\\nis characterised by a certain direction of flow, calling\\r\\nfor directed graphs; instead in this paper we consider\\r\\nan undirected graph. Using undirected graphs was shown\\r\\nto yield satisfactory results for pressure reconstruction\\r\\nin Hajgató et al. (2021) and will thus be adapted here\\r\\nas it has more applicability for real-life situations. As\\r\\nnovel contribution in the graph construction, we introduce\\r\\nself-loops for the node sensors. This increases the node\\r\\ndegree of pressure sensor nodes in the WDN, amplifying\\r\\nthe influence of the sensor nodes during reconstruction.\\r\\nExperimentally, higher node degrees graph yield to lower\\r\\ndetection errors than graphs without self-loops.\\r\\nWhile G captures the topological structure of the network,\\r\\nthe relation of a given junction to its neighbours is de-\\r\\nscribed by the adjacency matrix, A. The latter reveals\\r\\nthe connectivity between the nodes of a graph, which can\\r\\nreflect multiple factors, such as the length of the pipe\\r\\nseparating them, its diameter, the pipe coefficient and\\r\\nthe direction of flow. Three primal weight assignments are\\r\\ninvestigated, namely, i) unweighted edges, ii) pipe length-\\r\\nbased weights and variants thereof, and iii) hydraulic loss-\\r\\nbased weights for the pipe segments.\\r\\nUnweighted edges is the most elementary approach, which\\r\\nassumes binary weight value assignment as Avu\\r\\n=\\r\\n1 if evu ∈E, 0 otherwise. For a pipe length weighted adja-\\r\\ncency matrix, Avu = lvu, if evu ∈E, 0 otherwise, with lvu\\r\\nbeing the length of the pipe between node u and node v.\\r\\nFinally, a hydraulic loss weighted graph assumes the edge\\r\\nweight as an estimated head, or energy loss for the pipe\\r\\nsegment calculated empirically using the Hazen-Williams\\r\\nequation, Avu = 4.727 · C1.852\\r\\nvu\\r\\n· d4.871\\r\\nvu\\r\\n/lvu, if evu ∈E, 0\\r\\notherwise( Hajgató et al. (2021)). The different modes of\\r\\nweight assignment are treated as a hyperparameter when\\r\\ntraining the GNNs, with pipe-length ones found to yield\\r\\nlowest reconstruction- and prediction errors.\\r\\nThe last step is to define the signal on the graph. Each\\r\\nnode holds a T-dimensional feature vector, which repre-\\r\\nsents the hydraulic state of the node v over time. The sig-\\r\\nnal observed at time t at the different nodes on the graph\\r\\nis denoted by X(t)\\r\\nG\\r\\n= [x(t)\\r\\n1 , x(t)\\r\\n2 , . . . , x(t)\\r\\nN ], with x(t)\\r\\nv\\r\\n=NaN\\r\\nif the data is not observed at that location at time t.\\r\\nHence, reconstructing and predicting the pressure mea-\\r\\nsurements in the WDN is cast as a problem of graph signal\\r\\nprocessing. Given a partly observed WDN, its hydraulic\\r\\nstate is estimated at every intersection of the network by\\r\\nprocessing the physical time-series signals in conjunction\\r\\nwith topological information about the piping system. This\\r\\neffectively describes a graph-signal reconstruction problem\\r\\nand can be addressed with the application of GNNs. In\\r\\nthe following section, we first introduce the field of GNNs,\\r\\nmotivating our architectural choices. We then conclude the\\r\\nsection with the proposed methodology.\\r\\n2.2 Graph Signal Reconstruction and Prediction\\r\\nGraph Neural Networks\\r\\nGNNs are neural network archi-\\r\\ntectures that can process geometrical data (non-Euclidean)\\r\\nwith the goal of extracting node features combined with\\r\\ngraph topology to solve tasks such as prediction, forecast-\\r\\ning and classification (Ortega et al. (2018)). In the litera-\\r\\nture, their architectures can be classified into spatial- and\\r\\nspectral-based GNNs. The former, implement a message-\\r\\npassing process along the graph at each graph-convolution\\r\\noperation, which corresponds to the multiplication of a\\r\\nconvolution kernel with the corresponding node feature\\r\\nvectors, followed by a sum or a mean rule. These method-\\r\\nologies are very simple and adapt to different network\\r\\ntopologies, however they suffer from a well known over-\\r\\nsmoothing problem. In the case of deep networks with\\r\\nmany hidden layers, the extracted features assimilate\\r\\nacross the graph. The latter, spectral-based architectures,\\r\\nbuild on multiple hidden layers, each one performing\\r\\nspectral convolutions, defined from a GSP point of view.\\r\\nGSP provides a notion of frequency and a graph Fourier\\r\\ntransform, allowing for filtering operation in the spectral\\r\\n(frequency) domain. As a consequence, a graph convolu-\\r\\ntional layer, can be written by a sum of filtered signals\\r\\nfollowed by an activation function, where each filter is\\r\\ndefined in the graph spectral domain (using an eigen-\\r\\ndecomposition of a graph Laplacian). These networks alle-\\r\\nviate the oversmoothing effect, while suffering from a large\\r\\ncomputational burden induced by the forward/inverse\\r\\ngraph Fourier transform. To simplify the computational\\r\\ncomplexity, some approaches based on parameterisation\\r\\nusing Chebyshev polynomials Defferrard et al. (2017) have\\r\\nbeen proposed, known as the ChebNet model.\\r\\nOur Architecture\\r\\nFor a very sparsely monitored WDN,\\r\\nas the one in our work, a deeper network, consisting of\\r\\nmore hidden layers, might be needed. At the same time,\\r\\nto ensure a localisation of the leakage, we need to preserve\\r\\nas much as local information as possible. Oversmoothing\\r\\nwould not allow us to preserve high-frequency (hence local)\\r\\ninformation, thus we adopt spectral-based architectures.\\r\\nOur initial ChebNet model is based on the one provided\\r\\nby Hajgató et al. (2021) for the WDN of Richmond, which\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 4 ===\\r\\n664\\r\\nGarðar Örn Garðarsson et al. / IFAC PapersOnLine 55-6 (2022) 661–666\\r\\nFig. 3. ChebNet model for graph signal reconstruction.\\r\\nThe predictor (Fig. 1) has the same structure but the\\r\\ninput channel chin, has a depth of 3 time periods.\\r\\nhas a similar number of junctions and pipes as the WDN\\r\\nwe benchmark our model on. The model consists solely of\\r\\nChebyshev convolutional layers, which are regularised by\\r\\nthe weight decay of the gradient descent optimiser. Layer\\r\\nweights are initialised by the Xavier normal distribution\\r\\n(Glorot and Bengio (2010)) and biases set to zero. The\\r\\nhidden layers are activated with a SiLU sigmoid linear\\r\\nunit (Hendrycks and Gimpel (2016)) and the output layer\\r\\nis passed through a sigmoid activation. The ADAM opti-\\r\\nmiser (Kingma and Ba (2015)) is used due to its properties\\r\\nof having an adaptive learning rate with momentum, that\\r\\nadjusts to the function to be optimised.\\r\\nAn exhaustive ablation study to optimise hyper parame-\\r\\nters was provided by Hajgató et al. (2021) leading to the\\r\\nfollowing architecture for the WDN of Richmond: 4 Cheby-\\r\\nshev spectral graph convolutional layers with the degree of\\r\\nthe polynomial in the hidden layers set to [K1, K2, K3, ] =\\r\\n[240, 120, 20], and filter sizes [F1, F2, F3] = [120, 60, 30].\\r\\nThis forms the basis of our architecture, depicted in Fig. 3.\\r\\nThe input channel size, chin, here refers to the depth of\\r\\nthe input; in the case of the reconstructor, this will then\\r\\nequal one, as a single input graph is used to generate the\\r\\noutput, whereas for the predictor, this will be equivalent\\r\\nto the T number of time step used to generate the out-\\r\\nput prediction. From this basis, we optimised the graph\\r\\nconstruction methodologies (weights assignment, signal\\r\\nscaling methodologies, and with or without self-loop for\\r\\nobserved nodes) as motivated in window\\r\\n\\r\\n        result = np.array(x_)                                           # Dump our lists to an np.array\\r\\n        \\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Return the results\\r\\n    return n_timesteps=None):\\r\\n    '''\\r\\n    Function for cleaning the pressure dataframes obtained by simulation of the\\r\\n    nominal system model supplied with the BattLeDIM competition.\\r\\n    The output format is suitable for ingestion by the GNN model.\\r\\n    \\r\\n    Parameters\\r\\n    ----------\\r\\n    pressure_df : pd.DataFrame\\r\\n        Pandas dataframe where:\\r\\n            columns (x) = nodes\\r\\n            index   (y) = observations\\r\\n    sensor_list : list of ints\\r\\n        A list of numerical values indicating the sensors nodal placement..\\r\\n    scaling : str\\r\\n        'standard' - standard scaling\\r\\n        'minmax'   - min/max scaling\\r\\n    mode : str\\r\\n        'sensor_mask' - A per timestep stacked feature output np.array as per <- n4, ... observed\\r\\n         0.0  , 0    <- n5, ... unknown\\r\\n         ...     ]   etc.\\r\\n         \\r\\n    if mode='n_timesteps'\\r\\n    \\r\\n    x : unknown\\r\\n         0.0  , 0.0  , 0.0  , ... , t-n    <- n3, ... unknown\\r\\n         22.43, 22.51, 23.41, ... , t-n    <- n4, ... observed\\r\\n         0.0  , 0.0  , 0.0  , ... , t-n    <- n5, ... unknown\\r\\n         ...     ]   etc.\\r\\n        \\r\\n    y : np.array(n_obs,n_nodes,2)\\r\\n        The complete pressure signal matrix w/ 'n' number of observations.\\r\\n        With this we may train the GNN in a supervised manner.\\r\\n        \\r\\n        y =\\r\\n        [21.57    <- n1, all values are observed\\r\\n         21.89    <- n2,\\r\\n         22.17    <- n3\\r\\n         22.43    <- n4\\r\\n         23.79    <- n5\\r\\n         ...  ]   etc.\\r\\n        \\r\\n    '''\\r\\n    # The number of nodes in the passed dataframe\\r\\n    n_nodes = len(pressure_df.columns)\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Rename the columns (n1, n2, ...) to numerical values (1, 2, ...)\\r\\n    pressure_df.columns = [number for number in range(1,n_nodes+1)]\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Perform scaling on the initial Pandas Dataframe for brevity\\r\\n    # This is less trivial than applying it on the later generated numpy arrays\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Standard scale:\\r\\n    if rescale == 'standard':\\r\\n        _avg        = pressure_df.stack().mean()        # Calc. avg. over entire df.\\r\\n        _std        = pressure_df.stack().std(ddof=0)   # Calc. std.. over entire df.\\r\\n        bias        = _avg                              # Avg. is the scaling bias\\r\\n        scale       = _std                              # Std.dev. is the scaling range\\r\\n        pressure_df = (pressure_df - bias) / scale      # Scale to range\\r\\n        \\r\\n    # Min/max scaling (normalising):\\r\\n    elif rescale == 'minmax':\\r\\n        _min        = min(pressure_df.min())            # Find the absolute minimum value\\r\\n        _max        = max(pressure_df.max())            # Find the absolute maximum value\\r\\n        _rng        = _max - _min                       # Calculate the difference between (range)\\r\\n        bias        = _min                              # Scaling bias is the min value\\r\\n        scale       = _rng                              # Scaling range is the min-max range\\r\\n        pressure_df = (pressure_df - bias) / scale      # Scale to range\\r\\n        \\r\\n    # Perform no scaling\\r\\n    else:\\r\\n        bias        = None\\r\\n        scale       = None\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # DataFrame where the index is the node number holding the sensor and the value is set to 1\\r\\n    sensor_df = pd.DataFrame(data=[1 for i in observed_nodes],index=observed_nodes)\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Filled single row of DataFrame with the complete number of nodes, the unmonitored nodes are set to 0\\r\\n    sensor_df = sensor_df.reindex(list(range(1,n_nodes+1)),fill_value=0)\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Find the number of rows in the DataFrame to be masked...\\r\\n    n_rows = len(pressure_df)\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # ... and complete a mask DataFrame, where all the observations to keep are set to 1 and the rest to 0\\r\\n    mask_df = sensor_df.T.append([sensor_df.T for i in range(n_rows-1)],ignore_index=True)\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Enforce matching indices of the two DataFrames to be broadcast together\\r\\n    mask_df.index = pressure_df.index\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Returns a (n_observations, n_nodes, 2) feature vector (x) where the 3rd dimension is a 0/1 mask\\r\\n    # of the observed nodes\\r\\n    if mode=='sensor_mask':\\r\\n        \\r\\n        # Generating the incomplete feature matrix (x)\\r\\n        x_mask = np.array(mask_df)\\r\\n        x_arr  = np.array(pressure_df.where(cond=mask_df==1,other = 0.0))\\r\\n        x      = np.stack((x_arr,x_mask),axis=2)\\r\\n\\r\\n        # Generating the complete label matrix (y)\\r\\n        y_arr  = np.array(pressure_df)\\r\\n        y      = np.stack((y_arr, ),axis=2)\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Returns a (n_observations, n_nodes, n_timesteps) feature vector (x) where the 3rd dimension\\r\\n    # is the timesteps t, t-1, t-2 ... t-n leading to the observation to be predicted, at t+1\\r\\n    if mode=='n_timesteps':\\r\\n        \\r\\n        x_df         = pressure_df.where(cond=mask_df==1,other = 0.0)   # The feature dataframe (missing observations)\\r\\n        y_df         = pressure_df                                      # The label dataframe (complete observation)\\r\\n        \\r\\n        if task == 'prediction':                                        # If we're doing prediction we set the\\r\\n            n_samples = len(x_df)                                       # no.of samples as length of DF\\r\\n            \\r\\n        elif task == 'reconstruction':                                  # If we're doing reconstruction we set the\\r\\n            n_samples = len(x_df)+1                                     # no.of samples as length of DF + 1 due to\\r\\n                                                                        # slicing\\r\\n                \\r\\n        window_start = 0                                                # Set the start/end of the rolling window\\r\\n        window_end   = n_timesteps                                      # to be used to retrieve t-n timesteps for x\\r\\n\\r\\n        x_ = []                                                         # Initialise temp x_ and y_ lists\\r\\n        y_ = []                                                         # to contain our features and vectors\\r\\n\\r\\n        for i in range(n_timesteps,n_samples):                          # For each training sample\\r\\n            x_arr = (x_df.iloc[window_start:window_end].to_numpy().T)   # Add the t-n partial pressure signals\\r\\n            x_.append(np.flip(x_arr,axis=1))                            # Flip the order so that t is at index 0\\r\\n                                                                        # t-1 is at index 1, and so on\\r\\n            \\r\\n            if task == 'prediction':                                    # For prediction\\r\\n                y_.append(y_df.iloc[i])                                 # Add complete observation at t+1 as label\\r\\n                \\r\\n            elif task == 'reconstruction':                              # For reconstruction\\r\\n                y_.append(y_df.iloc[i-1])                               # Add complete observation at t as label\\r\\n            \\r\\n            window_start+=1                                             # Increment the\\r\\n            window_end  +=1                                             # rolling window\\r\\n\\r\\n        x = np.array(x_)                                                # Dump our lists\\r\\n        y = np.array(y_)                                                # to arrays\\r\\n        \\r\\n        row,col = y.shape                                               # Reshape the label array y\\r\\n        shape   = (row,col,1)                                           # so its dimensions are (n_observations, 1)\\r\\n        y = y.reshape(shape)                                            # not (n_observations, )\\r\\n        \\r\\n    return x,y,scale,bias                                               # Return the features, labels, scale & bias\\r\\n    \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n# Function that embeds the x-y labels on the graph and returns a DataLoader obj.\\r\\ndef dataGenerator(G, features, labels, batch_size, drop_last):\\r\\n    '''\\r\\n    Function from_networkx(G)  # Turn graph into tensor\\r\\n        graph.x = torch.Tensor(x)   # Embed features on graph\\r\\n        graph.y = torch.Tensor(y)   # Embed labels to the graph\\r\\n        data.append(graph)          # Append the tensorised graph reps to the data list\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Generate a sampler for picking sequential mini-batches at random from dataset\\r\\n    sampler   = RandomBatchSampler(data, batch_size, drop_last=False)\\r\\n    \\r\\n    # Construct a generator (data loader) from the list of graphs\\r\\n    generator = DataLoader(data, \\r\\n                           batch_sampler = sampler)\\r\\n                           #batch_size = batch_size, \\r\\n                           #shuffle    = shuffle)\\r\\n    \\r\\n    return generator\\r\\n\\r\\ndef DESCRIPTION.\\r\\n\\r\\n    '''\\r\\n    # Generate a torch tensor object from the networkx graph\\r\\n    graph  = from_networkx(G)\\r\\n    # Embed the signal to the x-features of the tensor\\r\\n often times been normalised or standardised\\r\\n    Given the scale and bias (e.g. mean and std in standard scaling, or min.\\r\\n    val and range during normalisation) the signal has been scaled as per:\\r\\n        (signal - bias) / scale\\r\\n    So, given a signal, bias and scale we may recalculate the initial signal \\r\\n    as per:\\r\\n        (signal * scale) + scale\\r\\n\\r\\n Given a partly observed WDN, its hydraulic\r\nstate is estimated at every intersection of the network by\r\nprocessing the physical time-series signals in conjunction\r\nwith topological information about the piping system. This\r\neffectively describes a graph-signal reconstruction problem\r\nand can be addressed with the application of GNNs. In\r\nthe following section, we first introduce the field of GNNs,\r\nmotivating our architectural choices. We then conclude the\r\nsection with the proposed methodology.\r\n2.2 Graph Signal Reconstruction and Prediction\r\nGraph Neural Networks\r\nGNNs are neural network archi-\r\ntectures that can process geometrical data (non-Euclidean)\r\nwith the goal of extracting node features combined with\r\ngraph topology to solve tasks such as prediction, forecast-\r\ning and classification (Ortega et al. (2018)). In the litera-\r\nture, their architectures can be classified into spatial- and\r\nspectral-based GNNs. The former, implement a message-\r\npassing process along the graph at each graph-convolution\r\noperation, which corresponds to the multiplication of a\r\nconvolution kernel with the corresponding node feature\r\nvectors, followed by a sum or a mean rule. These method-\r\nologies are very simple and adapt to different network\r\ntopologies, however they suffer from a well known over-\r\nsmoothing problem. In the case of deep networks with\r\nmany hidden layers, the extracted features assimilate\r\nacross the graph. The latter, spectral-based architectures,\r\nbuild on multiple hidden layers, each one performing\r\nspectral convolutions, defined from a GSP point of view.\r\nGSP provides a notion of frequency and a graph Fourier\r\ntransform, allowing for filtering operation in the spectral\r\n(frequency) domain. As a consequence, a graph convolu-\r\ntional layer, can be written by a sum of filtered signals\r\nfollowed by an activation function, where each filter is\r\ndefined in the graph spectral domain (using an eigen-\r\ndecomposition of a graph Laplacian). These networks alle-\r\nviate the oversmoothing effect, while suffering from a large\r\ncomputational burden induced by the forward/inverse\r\ngraph Fourier transform. To simplify the computational\r\ncomplexity, some approaches based on parameterisation\r\nusing Chebyshev polynomials Defferrard et al. (2017) have\r\nbeen proposed, known as the ChebNet model.\r\nOur Architecture\r\nFor a very sparsely monitored WDN,\r\nas the one in our work, a deeper network, consisting of\r\nmore hidden layers, might be needed. At the same time,\r\nto ensure a localisation of the leakage, we need to preserve\r\nas much as local information as possible. Oversmoothing\r\nwould not allow us to preserve high-frequency (hence local)\r\ninformation, thus we adopt spectral-based architectures.\r\nOur initial ChebNet model is based on the one provided\r\nby Hajgató et al. (2021) for the WDN of Richmond, which\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 4 ===\r\n664\r\nGarðar Örn Garðarsson et al. / IFAC PapersOnLine 55-6 (2022) 661–666\r\nFig. 3. ChebNet model for graph signal reconstruction.\r\nThe predictor (Fig. 1) has the same structure but the\r\ninput channel chin, has a depth of 3 time periods.\r\nhas a similar number of junctions and pipes as the WDN\r\nwe benchmark our model on. Layer\r\nweights are initialised by the Xavier normal distribution\r\n(Glorot and Bengio (2010)) and biases set to zero. The\r\nhidden layers are activated with a SiLU sigmoid linear\r\nunit (Hendrycks and Gimpel (2016)) and the output layer\r\nis passed through a sigmoid activation. The ADAM opti-\r\nmiser (Kingma and Ba (2015)) is used due to its properties\r\nof having an adaptive learning rate with momentum, that\r\nadjusts to the function to be optimised.\r\nAn exhaustive ablation study to optimise hyper parame-\r\nters was provided by Hajgató et al. From this basis, we optimised the graph\r\nconstruction methodologies (weights assignment, signal\r\nscaling methodologies, and with or without self-loop for\r\nobserved nodes) as motivated in Sec. 2.1, and the window\r\nsize for the predictor.\r\nDetection and Localisation Methodology\r\nWe now for-\r\nmalise the proposed method with Algorithm 1 and focus\r\non i) the estimation error, ii) the conversion from node\r\nto edge error (or residual), iii) fault detection and leakage\r\nlocalisation.\r\nNode Estimation Error Evaluation\r\nThe outputs of the\r\npredictor and reconstructor are compared, by evaluating\r\nat each iteration the difference between the reconstructed\r\npressure at time t with the predicted one, which was\r\ngenerated at t −1 using past information. The difference\r\nis the prediction error or residual signal, namely r(t) =\r\n[r1(t), r2(t), . . . , rN(t)] = ˆy(t)\r\np\r\n−ˆy(t)\r\nr . Under nominal non-\r\nfaulty conditions, the residual error should have stationary\r\nbehavior and should be caused mainly by the presence\r\nof random noise. When this residual does not display a\r\nstationary behavior anymore, this implies a change in the\r\nunderlying model describing the behaviour of the WDN.\r\nIn this paper, we identify this change as the presence of a\r\nleakage.\r\nAlgorithm 1 Leak detection pseudocode\r\n1: for every_timestep : t do\r\n2:\r\nreadings ←readPressureSensors()\r\n3:\r\nif first_run then\r\n4:\r\nnext_prediction ←GNN_1.predict(readings)\r\n5:\r\nelse\r\n6:\r\n// Calculate prediction error\r\n7:\r\nlast_prediction ←next_prediction\r\n8:\r\nreconstruction ←GNN_2.predict(readings)\r\n9:\r\nnode_err ←reconstruction −last_prediction\r\n10:\r\n// Convert nodal error to edge error\r\n11:\r\nfor connected_nodes : i, j in node_error do\r\n12:\r\nedge_err ←node_err(i) −node_err(j)\r\n13:\r\nend for\r\n14:\r\n// Assess fault condition\r\n15:\r\nfor each_edge : v in edge_err do\r\n16:\r\nthresh ←α × edge_err(v).rolling(m).std()\r\n17:\r\nmv_avg ←|edge_err(v).rolling(m).mean()|\r\n18:\r\nif mv_avg > thresh then\r\n19:\r\nLeak detected, log edge, v.\r\n20:\r\nend if\r\n21:\r\nend for\r\n22:\r\n// Filter duplicate alarms\r\n23:\r\nfor faulty_edges : v do\r\n24:\r\nfor neighbour in k-hop distance of v do\r\n25:\r\nif neighbour has fault in the past n steps then\r\n26:\r\nSuppress fault alarms from edge\r\n27:\r\nend if\r\n28:\r\nend for\r\n29:\r\nend for\r\n30:\r\nnext_prediction ←GNN_1.predict(readings)\r\n31:\r\nend if\r\n32:\r\nRaise alarm for edges v that are in alarm state\r\n33: end for\r\nEdge error evaluation\r\nInstead of considering the residual\r\nsignal at each node, a novel way to process the residuals\r\nis proposed, whereby the signals are considered on a per-\r\nedge basis, rather than a node one. This stems from the\r\nnotion that leaks commonly happen along a pipe rather\r\nthan at a junction, and for it being directly applicable\r\nto the benchmark dataset. In the case of an edge euv,\r\nconnecting the two neighbouring nodes v and u, we would\r\nconsider its residual as r(E)\r\nuv (t) as r(E)\r\nuv (t) = rv(t) −ru(t).\r\nAssess fault condition. Once the edge error is evaluated,\r\nwe need to detect possible faulty conditions. The strategy\r\nconsists of calculating the moving average of the residual\r\nsignal r(E)\r\nuv (t) of a given edge uv in the network, for a\r\nwindow of size m at observation time t, 1 as per ¯r(E)\r\nuv (t) =\r\n1/m \u001fm−1\r\nj=0 r(E)\r\nuv (t −j) and in defining a detection logic as:\r\nFD =\r\n\r\n\r\n0\r\nif |¯r(E)\r\nuv (t)| < ¯ρuv(t)\r\n1\r\notherwise\r\n(1)\r\nwhere, ¯ρuv(t) = α¯σuv(t), and α is a parameter that allows\r\nto tune the acceptable false alarm rate based on Chebyshev\r\ninequalities, with ¯σ(t)\r\nuv being the rolling standard deviation\r\nof the residual signal for a window of size m. This test\r\nis then individually applied on each residual signal at\r\nevery time step to obtain a classification of whether the\r\nsignal is deemed indicative of a leak being present in its\r\nneighbourhood or not.\r\n1 For the sake of notation simplicity, we omit the dependence on m.\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 1 ===\r\nHonda Research Institute Europe GmbH\r\nhttps://www.honda-ri.de/\r\nSAMknn Regressor for Online Learning in Water\r\nDistribution Networks\r\nJonathan Jakob, André Artelt, Martina Hasenjäger,\r\nBarbara Hammer\r\n2022\r\nPreprint:\r\nThis is a post-peer-review, pre-copyedit version of an article published in\r\nInternational Conference on Artificial Neural Networks (ICANN) 2022. The final\r\nauthenticated version is available online at:\r\nhttps://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-15934-3_62\r\nPowered by TCPDF (www.tcpdf.org)\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 2 ===\r\nSAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning\r\nin Water Distribution Networks\r\nJonathan Jakob1, Andr´e Artelt1,3(B), Martina Hasenj¨ager2,\r\nand Barbara Hammer1\r\n1 Bielefeld University, Bielefeld, Germany\r\naartelt@techfak.uni-bielefeld.de\r\n2 Honda Research Institute, Oﬀenbach, Germany\r\n3 University of Cyprus, Nicosia, Cyprus\r\nAbstract. Water distribution networks are a key component of modern\r\ninfrastructure for housing and industry. They transport and distribute\r\nwater via widely branched networks from sources to consumers. In order\r\nto guarantee a working network at all times, the water supply company\r\ncontinuously monitors the network and takes actions when necessary –\r\ne.g. reacting to leakages, sensor faults and drops in water quality. Since\r\nreal world networks are too large and complex to be monitored by a\r\nhuman, algorithmic monitoring systems have been developed. A popular\r\ntype of such systems are residual based anomaly detection systems that\r\ncan detect events such as leakages and sensor faults. For a continuous\r\nhigh quality monitoring, it is necessary for these systems to adapt to\r\nchanged demands and presence of various anomalies.\r\nIn this work, we propose an adaption of the incremental SAM-kNN\r\nclassifier for regression to build a residual based anomaly detection sys-\r\ntem for water distribution networks that is able to adapt to any kind of\r\nchange.\r\nKeywords: SAM-kNN regressor · Incremental · Anomaly detection\r\n1\r\nIntroduction\r\nWater is the foundation of (our) life – we need water for drinking, cooking,\r\nhygiene and farming. Water distribution networks (WDNs), which distribute\r\nwater from the supplier to the customers, are therefore considered as critical\r\ninfrastructure. A major problem for water utility companies (and society in\r\ngeneral) are anomalies that cause loss or contamination of water – e.g. leakages\r\nsuch as pipe bursts, sensor faults, pollution, cyber-physical attacks, etc. [1,3,15].\r\nJ. Jakob and A. Artelt—Contributed equally.\r\nWe gratefully acknowledge funding from the VW-Foundation for the project IMPACT\r\nfunded in the frame of the funding line AI and its Implications for Future Society, and\r\nfunding from the European Research Council (ERC) under the ERC Synergy Grant\r\nWater-Futures (Grant agreement No. 951424).\r\nc\r\n⃝The Author(s), under exclusive license to Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2022\r\nE. Pimenidis et al. (Eds.): ICANN 2022, LNCS 13531, pp. 752–762, 2022.\r\nhttps://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-15934-3_62\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 3 ===\r\nSAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning in Water Distribution Networks\r\n753\r\nBecause of water shortages, among others caused by climate change, (drinking)\r\nwater becomes an increasingly valuable resource that should not be wasted.\r\nHowever, it was estimated that leakages in WDNs lead to a loss of more than\r\n45 million m3 of drinking water in developing countries – even highly developed\r\ncountries such as the island of Cyprus looses approx. up to 25% of their drinking\r\nwater due to leaky pipes [11].\r\nBecause of the increasing availability of sensors (e.g. pressure sensors)\r\nin WDNs, water utility companies nowadays use computer systems for\r\n(autonomously) monitoring their networks [14]. These systems are realized using\r\nmethods from engineering, statistics and machine learning (ML) [2]. While many\r\ndiﬀerent and successful methods for anomaly detection and localization have\r\nbeen proposed [17], these methods are usually not able to adapt to an occurring\r\nchange or anomaly. These systems might be able to detect anomalies but once\r\nthe anomaly is detected, they are “blind” for everything else that happens while\r\nthe detected anomaly is present – the systems must be recalibrated or refitted\r\nwhich becomes challenging because a large amount of data (and therefore col-\r\nlection time) is needed. Adaptation to changes – in case of WDNs anomalies or\r\nsimply changes in the water consumption behavior of the customers (i.e. changed\r\ndemand) – can be natural handled by online learning methods.\r\nOnline learning [4] can be considered as a sub-field of machine learning which\r\ndeals with models that are trained incrementally – i.e. they can learn from a data\r\nstream instead of a fixed training set only. For example, they can be used for\r\nelectricity price prediction [20] or electric load forecasting [19].\r\nIn this work, we contribute to online learning for regression problems and\r\nwater distribution networks as a particular field of application. More specifically,\r\nour contributions are:\r\n– We propose SAM-kNN regression, a memory based online learner for regres-\r\nsion problems.\r\n– We evaluate our proposed SAM-kNN regression method in the context of\r\nanomaly detection in water distribution networks.\r\nThe remainder of this paper is structured as follows: After briefly reviewing\r\nrelated work in Sect. 2, we introduce the problem setting we are considering in\r\nthis work (see Sect. 3). Next, in Sect. 4 we propose SAM-kNN regression for online\r\nlearning, which we empirically evaluate in the context of anomaly detection\r\nin water distribution networks (see Sect. 5). Finally, this work closes with a\r\nsummary and conclusion in Sect. 6.\r\n2\r\nRelated Work\r\nIncremental or online learning is a machine learning paradigm in which a model\r\nis updated after each data sample that is fed into it. This paradigm is especially\r\nsuited for large data sets, that are too big to be processed in batch fashion, or\r\nin situations where data becomes available only sample after sample – e.g. in\r\nthe form of a potentially infinite stream of data. Incremental learning has made\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 4 ===\r\n754\r\nJ. Jakob et al.\r\ngreat strides in recent years [5], with most applications set in a classification\r\nenvironment [7,9,10,12,18,21].\r\nOne particular system is the SAM-kNN classifier [13]. This incremental algo-\r\nrithm was created to perform on long data streams, using its internal memory\r\nstructure to alleviate the problem of catastrophic forgetting when frequent con-\r\ncept changes are expected.\r\nOn the other hand, only a few approaches that utilize incremental learning for\r\nregression problems exist. In [19] the authors use an incremental variant of the\r\nsupport vector regressor (SVR) to build models for electricity price prediction.\r\n[20] uses a similar SVR, paired with phase space reconstruction for time series to\r\nfacilitate electrical load forecasting. In [6] a wide range of incremental regression\r\nalgorithms are compared for their use in exoskeleton control.\r\nHowever, all of these publications utilize standard incremental algorithms\r\nand do not explicitly build a new model to work with. We, on the other hand,\r\npropose a reformulation of the SAM-kNN classifier for regression as a standalone\r\nalgorithm.\r\n3\r\nProblem Setting\r\nIncremental or online regression is the task of predicting a response variable\r\ny ∈R from a stream S = {x1, x2, x3, ...} of variables X ∈Rn. Hereby, a new\r\ninstance of the incremental model is learned for each incoming sample of the\r\ndata stream.\r\nWe work on water distribution networks that have several internal nodes\r\nn. These nodes are equipped with sensors, that measure water pressure and\r\nflow rate. Each sensor provides read outs at specific time intervals t. For\r\nevery node, this creates a potentially infinite data stream of sensors values\r\nS = {(s1, y1), (s2, y2), (s3, y3), ...}, where si ∈Rn−1 represents the sensor values\r\nof all but one node in the network and the predictor variable yi ∈R represents\r\nthe sensor value at the remaining node.\r\nThis means, that we use the read outs of n −1 nodes to predict the value of\r\nthe nth node using an incremental regression algorithm. Said algorithm processes\r\nthe stream S instance after instance by generating a sequence of models H =\r\n{h1, h2, h3, ...}, where hi−1(si) = ˆyi. After each prediction the true value yi is\r\nrevealed and a new model hi is learned.\r\nWe use the Interleaved train test error (ITTE) as a cost function to train the\r\nmodel:\r\nE(S) =\r\n\u0002\r\n\u0003\r\n\u0003\r\n\u00041\r\nt\r\nt\r\n\u0005\r\ni=1\r\n(hi−1(si) −yi)2\r\n(1)\r\nThis ITTE measures the Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE) over every model\r\nhi up to a given time point t.\r\nWhenever the local error hi−1(si)−yi exceeds a certain threshold, this means,\r\nthat more water than predicted flows through the observed node. This is taken as\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 5 ===\r\nSAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning in Water Distribution Networks\r\n755\r\nan indication for a water leak and an alarm will be triggered. Being an incremen-\r\ntal algorithm, our model will then automatically adjust to the new circumstances\r\nso that accurate prediction of the water flow will be maintained throughout the\r\nleak. Our model, which will be explained in detail in the next section, has the\r\ncapability to remember long term concepts and therefore, as soon as the water\r\nleak is fixed, it will revert back to the normal circumstances.\r\n4\r\nModel\r\nOur proposed model is an adaption of the Self Adjusting Memory (SAM) [13],\r\nan incremental classifier, to regression. This approach is based on two distinct\r\ninternal memories, the Short-Term (STM) and the Long-Term memory (LTM).\r\nHereby, the STM is a dynamic sliding window over the last m samples, that is\r\nsupposed to only hold the most recent concept of the data stream:\r\nMST = {(xi, yi) ∈Rn × R | i = t −m + 1, ..., t}\r\n(2)\r\nThe LTM, on the other hand, is a collection of p samples, which hold older\r\nconcepts, that do not contradict the STM and might still be of use in the future:\r\nMLT = {(xi, yi) ∈Rn × R | i = 1, ..., p}\r\n(3)\r\nAdditionally, there is the combined memory (CM), which is a simple union of\r\nthe STM and the LTM:\r\nMC = MST ∪MLT\r\n(4)\r\nEach memory induces a kNN regressor that can be used independently from the\r\nothers. To determine which kNN is used for every new incoming data sample,\r\nthe ITTE (see Sect. 3) is tracked for all sub-models and the one with the lowest\r\ncurrent ITTE is chosen.\r\n4.1\r\nModel Parameters\r\nThe proposed model has three parameters that are continuously adapted during\r\ndeployment:\r\n1. The size m of the STM sliding window\r\n2. The data samples in the LTM\r\n3. The ITTEs of the three sub-models\r\nAdditionally, there are three hyperparameters that can be chosen robustly and\r\nare set before deployment:\r\n1. The number of neighbours k\r\n2. The minimum size Lmin of the STM\r\n3. The maximum size Lmax of the LTM\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 6 ===\r\n756\r\nJ. Jakob et al.\r\n4.2\r\nModel Adaption\r\nWhenever a new data sample arrives, it is added to the STM, which means\r\nthat this memory grows continuously. However, since it is supposed to hold only\r\nthe most recent concept, a reduction of the STM window size is performed on a\r\nregular basis. This is facilitated by testing smaller window sizes at every iteration\r\nand choosing the one that is optimizing the ITTE. Tested windows are:\r\nMl = {(xt−l+1, yt−l+1), ..., (xt, yt)}\r\n(5)\r\nwhere l ∈{m, m/2, m/4...} and l ≥Lmin.\r\nMSTt+1 =\r\nargmin\r\nS∈{Mm…591 tokens truncated…\r\nchosen for every iteration of the discarding process.\r\n4.5\r\nFinal Model\r\nThe complete pseudocode of our proposed model is given in Algorithm 1.\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 8 ===\r\n758\r\nJ. Jakob et al.\r\nAlgorithm 1. SAM-kNN Regression\r\nInput: Data stream S, one si at a time\r\nOutput: ˆyi for every si\r\n1: MST , MLT = {s0, ..., sLmin}\r\n▷Initialize STM and LTM\r\n2: EST , ELT , EC = 0\r\n▷Initialize tracked errors\r\n3: for si ∈S \\ {s0, ..., sLmin} do\r\n▷Loop over the remaining data stream\r\n4:\r\nBM = argmin(EST , ELT , EC)\r\n▷Find best memory with lowest error\r\n5:\r\nˆyi = kNNBM(si)\r\n▷Predict with kNN of best memory\r\n6:\r\nUpdate EST , ELT , EC\r\n7:\r\nMST = MST ∪{si}\r\n▷Add current sample to STM\r\n8:\r\nEvaluate smaller STM sizes\r\n9:\r\nif STM is reduced then\r\n10:\r\nOt = MSTt \\ MSTt+1\r\n▷Take discarded samples from STM\r\n11:\r\nclean(Ot, MSTt+1)\r\n▷Clean discarded samples with respect to new STM\r\n12:\r\nMLT = MLT ∪clean(Ot, MSTt+1)\r\n▷Add cleaned samples to LTM\r\n13:\r\nclean(MLT , MSTt+1)\r\n▷Clean new LTM with respect to new STM\r\n14:\r\nend if\r\n15: end for\r\n5\r\nExperiments\r\nWe empirically evaluate our proposed method in an online scenario for detecting\r\nleakages and sensor faults in water distribution networks – all experiments are\r\nimplemented in Python1.\r\n5.1\r\nData\r\nWe use a version of the L-Town water distribution network as used in [16], as a\r\nprominent realistic benchmark for anomaly detection – we only use Area A which\r\nconsists of 661 nodes, 766 links, and 29 (optimally placed) pressure sensors. We\r\nbuild and simulate 10 scenarios where the first 5 scenarios each contain a single\r\nleakage – we vary position, time and size of the leakage – and the remaining 5\r\nscenarios each contain a single diﬀerent sensor fault (position is varied):\r\n– Scenario 6: Sensor measurement is overflowing over time – i.e. it is going to\r\ninfinity over time.\r\n– Scenario 7: Gaussian noise is added to the sensor measurement.\r\n– Scenario 8: A constant value is added to the sensor measurement.\r\n– Scenario 9: Sensor measurement is set equal to zero.\r\n– Scenario 10: Sensor measurement is shifted by a small amount.\r\nEach scenario is simulated (using WNTR [8]) for 3 months and pressure sensors\r\nare sampled every 5 min.\r\n1 Implementation is available on GitHub: https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-\r\nkNN-Regressor OnlineLearning WDNs.\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 9 ===\r\nSAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning in Water Distribution Networks\r\n759\r\nFig. 1. L-Town network [16] – we only use “Area A” where we have 29 pressure sensors.\r\nThe data stream of sensor measurements is post processed by using a sliding\r\nwindow of size 4 – we average all samples dimensional wise in this time window,\r\nso that we end up with 29 dimensional samples.\r\n5.2\r\nSetup\r\nWe compare our proposed SAM-kNN regressor (see Sect. 4) to several other\r\nonline learning regressors. In order to justify the introduced overhead of the SAM\r\narchitecture, we compare its performance to vanilla regressors (kNN regression\r\nand linear regression) wrapped as online learners by using the river toolbox2.\r\nFor each pressure sensor, we build a corresponding virtual sensor based on\r\nall other pressure sensors – i.e. we try to predict (using a regressor) the pressure\r\nbased on the past pressure values of all other pressure sensors (see Sect. 3).\r\nThese virtual sensors are then used for a residual based anomaly detection –\r\ni.e. an alarm (detected anomaly) is raised when the predicted pressure value\r\ndeviates too much from the observed pressure measurement. For each scenario,\r\nthe processed data stream is fed as batches of 200 samples to the regressors\r\n(realizing the virtual sensors).\r\nFor each regressor, we evaluate the performance of the resulting anomaly\r\ndetector – since we are interested in the detection of a single anomaly (leakage\r\nand sensor fault), we report true positives (TP), false positives (FP) and false\r\nnegatives (FN). Note that the true positives and false negatives are always either\r\n0 or 1 because we only check whether an alarm was raised when the single\r\nanomaly was present or not – however, for the false positives, we count every\r\nsingle false alarm.\r\n2 https://github.com/online-ml/river.\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 10 ===\r\n760\r\nJ. Jakob et al.\r\n5.3\r\nResults\r\nThe results for leakage detection, for each regressor and each scenario, are shown\r\nin Table 1. Likewise, the results for sensor fault detection are shown in Table 2.\r\nWe observe the same/similar eﬀects for both types of anomalies (leakages and\r\nsensor faults): We observe that linear regression completely fails to detect the\r\nanomaly – this indicates that a linear model is not suﬃcient to model the\r\nhydraulic dynamics in the water distribution network and hence fails to detect\r\nany anomalies. There is only one exception: For scenario 6 the linear model is\r\nable to detect the sensor fault – recall that in this particular scenario the sen-\r\nsor fault is characterized by a slowly overflowing sensor measurements (i.e. the\r\npressure value goes to infinity), which is expected to be easily detected because\r\nit is a very “loud” fault. The kNN model shows good performance in detecting\r\nthe anomaly but has a huge number of false positives – i.e. it is too sensitive and\r\nraises lots of false alarms. Our proposed SAM-kNN shows the best performance\r\n– it is able to consistently detect the anomalies while having a small number\r\nof false positives only. The huge reduction of the false positives in comparison\r\nTable 1. In contrast to other online learners, our proposed\r\nmethod comes with a memory component which allows it to remember past\r\nconcepts quite easily. We empirically evaluated our proposed online learner in\r\nan anomaly detection scenario for a realistic water distribution network – our\r\nproposed online learner consistently outperforms other standard online learners.\r\nAlthough our proposed method shows good performance for leakage and\r\nsensor fault detection (two very common anomalies), it is unclear, how well it\r\nperforms for other (more complex) types of anomalies such as cyber-physical\r\nattacks, etc. Furthermore, another challenge is high-dimensional data – in this\r\nwork our data had 29 dimensions which is already somewhat high but can still be\r\nmanaged by our kNN based method. However, in case of really high dimensional\r\ndata, kNN will encounter performance problems – e.g. some kind of integrated\r\ndimensionality reduction might be required. We leave these aspects as future\r\nwork.\r\nAcknowledgment. We acknowledge the bachelor thesis by Augustin Harter (Biele-\r\nfeld University) and Yannik Sander (Bielefeld University) which served as a mental\r\nstarting point for this work.\r\nReferences\r\n1. Zhu, Q., He, Z., Ye, X.: Incremental classifier learning based on PEDCC-loss and\r\ncosine distance (2019). https://arxiv.org/abs/1906.04734\r\n#!/usr/bin/env python3\r\n# -*- coding: utf-8 -*-\r\n\"\"\"\r\nCreated on Tue Aug 24 14:32:17 2021\r\n\r\n@author: gardar\r\n\"\"\"\r\n\r\nimport os\r\nimport yaml\r\nimport time\r\nimport torch\r\nimport epynet\r\nimport numpy as np\r\nimport pandas as pd\r\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\r\nfrom sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split\r\n\r\nfrom utils.epanet_loader import get_nx_graph\r\nfrom utils.epanet_simulator import epanetSimulator\r\nfrom utils.data_loader import battledimLoader, dataCleaner, dataGenerator, embedSignalOnGraph, rescaleSignal\r\nfrom modules.torch_gnn import ChebNet\r\nfrom utils.visualisation import visualise\r\n\r\n\r\ndef predict_pressure(graph, pressure_series, print_out_rate=100, save=True, filename='predictions.csv'):\r\n    \r\n    results = []\r\n    elapsed_time = time.time()\r\n    \r\n    for i, partial_graph_signal in enumerate(pressure_series):\r\n        if not i % print_out_rate:\r\n            execution_time = time.time()\r\n        \r\n        results.append(model.predict(graph, partial_graph_signal))\r\n        \r\n        if not i % print_out_rate:\r\n            print('Signal:\\t{}\\t Execution:\\t{:.3f} s\\t Elapsed:\\t{:.3f} s'.format(i,\r\n                                                                                   time.time()-execution_time, \r\n                                                                                   time.time()-elapsed_time))\r\n    if save:\r\n        print('-'*63+\"\\nSaving results to: \\n{}/{}\\n\\n\".format(os.getcwd(),filename))\r\n        pd.DataFrame(results).to_csv(filename)\r\n    \r\n    return results\r\n\r\n\r\nif __name__ == '__main__':\r\n    \r\n    '''\r\n     0. Configure Runtime\r\n    '''\r\n    print('+' + '-'*63 + '+')\r\n    print('|' + ' '*63 + '|')\r\n    print('|' + '{:^63s}'.format(\"P R E D I C T . P Y\") + '|')\r\n    print('|' + ' '*63 + '|')\r\n    print('+' + '-'*63 + '+')\r\n    print('\\n')\r\n    \r\n    # Runtime configuration\r\n    path_to_wdn     = './data/L-TOWN.inp'\r\n    path_to_data    = './data/l-town-data/'\r\n    weight_mode     = 'pipe_length'\r\n    self_loops      = True\r\n    scaling         = 'minmax'\r\n    task            = 'prediction'\r\n    mode            = 'n_timesteps'\r\n    n_timesteps     = 3\r\n    figsize         = (50,16)\r\n    print_out_rate  = 100               \r\n    model_name      = 'l-town-chebnet-' + task +'-' + mode + '-' + 'n_{}'.format(n_timesteps)\r\n    last_model_path = './studies/models/' + model_name + '-1.pt'\r\n    last_log_path   = './studies/logs/'   + model_name + '-1.csv'\r\n        \r\n    '''\r\n     1. Make Graph\r\n    '''\r\n    print('1. Making graph from EPANET input file \\n')\r\n    \r\n    # Import the .inp file using the EPYNET library\r\n    wdn = epynet.Network(path_to_wdn)\r\n    \r\n    # Solve hydraulic model for a single timestep\r\n    wdn.solve()\r\n    \r\n    # Convert the file using a custom function, based on:\r\n    # https://github.com/BME-SmartLab/GraphConvWat \r\n    G , pos , head = get_nx_graph(wdn, weight_mode=weight_mode, get_head=True)\r\n    \r\n    # Open the dataset configuration file\r\n    with open(path_to_data + 'dataset_configuration.yml') as file:\r\n    \r\n        # Load the configuration to a dictionary\r\n        config = yaml.load(file, Loader=yaml.FullLoader) \r\n    \r\n    # Generate a list of integers, indicating the number of the node\r\n    # at which a  pressure sensor is present\r\n    sensors = [int(string.replace(\"n\", \"\")) for string in config['pressure_sensors']]\r\n    \r\n    if self_loops:\r\n        for sensor_node in sensors:             # For each node in the sensor list\r\n            G.add_edge(u_of_edge=sensor_node,   # Add an edge from that node ...\r\n                       v_of_edge=sensor_node,   # ... to itself ...\r\n                       weight=1.,name='SELF')   # ... and set its weight to equal 1\r\n    \r\n    '''\r\n     2. Get Simulation Data\r\n    '''\r\n    print('2. Retrieving simulation data \\n')\r\n    \r\n    # Instantiate the nominal WDN model\r\n    nominal_wdn_model = epanetSimulator(path_to_wdn, path_to_data)\r\n    \r\n    # Run a simulation\r\n    nominal_wdn_model.simulate()\r\n    \r\n    # Retrieve the nodal pressures\r\n    nominal_pressure = nominal_wdn_model.get_simulated_pressure()\r\n    \r\n    x,y,scale,bias = dataCleaner(pressure_df    = nominal_pressure, # Pass the nodal pressures\r\n                                 observed_nodes = sensors,          # Indicate which nodes have sensors\r\n                                 rescale        = scaling,          # Perform scaling on the timeseries data\r\n                                 mode           = mode,             # Set data curation mode (n_timesteps / sensor_mask)\r\n                                 task           = task,             # Set data curation task (prediction  / reconstruction)\r\n                                 n_timesteps    = n_timesteps)      # Perform scaling on the timeseries data\r\n    \r\n    # Split the data into training and validation sets\r\n    x_trn, x_val, y_trn, y_val = train_test_split(x, y, \r\n                                                  test_size    = 0.2,\r\n                                                  random_state = 1,\r\n                                                  shuffle      = False)\r\n    \r\n    '''\r\n     3. Get Historical Data\r\n    '''\r\n    print('3. Retrieving historical data \\n')\r\n    \r\n    # Load the data into a numpy array with format matching the GraphConvWat problem\r\n    pressure_2018 = battledimLoader(observed_nodes = sensors,\r\n                                    n_nodes        = 782,\r\n                                    path           = path_to_data,\r\n                                    file           = '2018_SCADA_Pressures.csv',\r\n                                    rescale        = True, \r\n                                    scale          = scale,\r\n                                    bias           = bias)\r\n    \r\n    pressure_2019 = battledimLoader(observed_nodes = sensors,\r\n                                    n_nodes        = 782,\r\n                                    path           = path_to_data,\r\n                                    file           = '2019_SCADA_Pressures.csv',\r\n                                    rescale        = True, \r\n                                    scale          = scale,\r\n                                    bias           = bias)\r\n    \r\n    '''\r\n     4. Load a Trained GNN Model\r\n    '''\r\n    print('4. Loading trained GNN model \\n')\r\n    \r\n    # Set the computation device as NVIDIA GPU if available else CPU\r\n    device = torch.device('cuda' if torch.cuda.is_available() else 'cpu')\r\n    \r\n    # Instantiate a Chebysev Network GNN model\r\n    model  = ChebNet(name           = 'ChebNet',\r\n                     data_generator = None,\r\n                     device         = device, \r\n                     in_channels    = np.shape(x_trn)[-1], \r\n                     out_channels   = np.shape(y_trn)[-1],\r\n                     data_scale     = scale, \r\n                     data_bias      = bias).to(device)\r\n    \r\n    # We offer the user the option to load the previously trained weights\r\n    model.load_model(last_model_path, last_log_path)\r\n    \r\n    '''\r\n     5. Predict a Year's worth of Data\r\n    '''\r\n    print('\\n')\r\n    print('5.1 Predicting 2018 pressure\\n')\r\n    \r\n    prediction_2018 = predict_pressure(G, \r\n                                       pressure_2018,\r\n                                       print_out_rate = print_out_rate, \r\n                                       save           = True, \r\n                                       filename       = '2018_predictions.csv')\r\n    \r\n    print('5.2 Predicting 2019 pressure\\n')\r\n    \r\n    prediction_2019 = predict_pressure(G, \r\n                                       pressure_2019,\r\n                                       print_out_rate = print_out_rate, \r\n                                       save           = True, \r\n                                       filename       = '2019_predictions.csv')\r\n    \r\n    print('+' + '-'*63 + '+')\r\n    print('|' + ' '*63 + '|')\r\n    print('|' + '{:^63s}'.format(\"E X E C U T I O N   C O M P L E T E D\") + '|')\r\n    print('|' + ' '*63 + '|')\r\n    print('+' + '-'*63 + '+')\r\n"
  }
]
