By leveraging a preprocessing model, our approach mounts the challenges posed by data\r\nsparsity and sensor heterogeneity limitations. Liquor-HGNN outperforms all other approaches on the\r\nsame dataset in terms of Economic score. Here, the Economic Score function iterates over the detected\r\nleakages, finds the closest pipes to each detected leakage, and calculates the score contribution for each\r\ntrue detection based on the detected distance as well as on the starting time of the leakages. To the best\r\nof our knowledge, Liquor-HGNN represents the first-ever application of a heterogeneous Graph Neural\r\nNetwork (GNN) specifically tailored for leak detection in DWDNs.\r\nKeywords\r\nDeep learning and representation learning, Temporal and spatio-temporal data analytics, Applications of\r\nmachine learning in engineering, Leakage detection and localization\r\n1. Introduction\r\nWater pipe systems are crucial for providing clean drinking water to communities, but they are\r\nprone to leaks and ruptures, leading to resource waste and damage [1]. Thus, detecting and\r\nlocating leaks in drinking water distribution networks (DWDNs) is essential for maintaining\r\nthe integrity and sustainability of these systems [2]. This paper introduces the Liquor-HGNN\r\nmodel, a novel approach that utilizes heterogeneous graph learning to accurately identify the\r\nstart time of leaks and to pinpoint leaky pipes in DWDNs.\r\n1.1. Background and Motivation\r\nLeakage in the water distribution network is a major source of non-revenue water. Non-revenue\r\nwater refers to the discrepancy between the volume of water supplied to the distribution network\r\nand the amount of water actually billed to customers. On average, non-revenue water accounts\r\nLWDA’23: Lernen, Wissen, Daten, Analysen. October 09–11, 2023, Marburg, Germany\r\n∗Corresponding author.\r\nEnvelope-Open schaller@informatik.uni-wuerzburg.de (M. Schaller); steiniger@informatik.uni-wuerzburg.de (M. Steininger);\r\ndulny@informatik.uni-wuerzburg.de (A. Dulny); schloer@informatik.uni-wuerzburg.de (D. Schlör);\r\nhotho@informatik.uni-wuerzburg.de (A. Hotho)\r\nGLOBE https://github.com/MilanShao/ (M. Schaller)\r\n© 2023 Copyright for this paper by its authors. Use permitted under Creative Commons License Attribution 4.0 International (CC BY 4.0).\r\nCEUR\r\nWorkshop\r\nProceedings\r\nhttp://ceur-ws.org\r\nISSN 1613-0073\r\nCEUR Workshop Proceedings (CEUR-WS.org)\r\nCEUR\r\nWorkshop\r\nProceedings\r\nceur-ws.org\r\nISSN 1613-0073\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 2 ===\r\nfor approximately 35 percent of the total water supply [3]. This highlights the pressing need\r\nfor efficient leak detection methods in order to prevent wastage of resources and maintain the\r\noverall quality and reliability of the water system.\r\n1.2. Problem Statement and Relevance\r\nFigure 1: Visualization of the underground pipelines with different sensor types (demand, pressure and\r\nflow), demand patterns (here dividing between residential and commercial demand), the data collection\r\nof GIS (Geo Information System) Coordinates and the Liquor-HGNN pipeline for leak detection and\r\nlocalization.\r\nTraditional visual inspection methods are impractical for underground pipelines, leading\r\nto the installation of sensors in water distribution networks [4]. However, due to financial\r\nand spatial limitations, these sensors are sparsely distributed across pipeline sections [5]. The\r\ncomplexity and diversity of sensor data (e.g. the different sorts of sensors, that are used to\r\nmeasure DWDNs like flow, demand or pressure sensors), along with the large distances between\r\nsensors (see pipe sections in Fig. 1) and different types of leaks, pose challenges in accurately\r\ndetermining the leaky pipe section and starting time of leaks. These different types of leaks\r\ninclude background leaks, incipient leakages, as well as abrupt leakages such as medium pipe\r\nbursts and large pipe bursts [6]. Additionally, the collected time-series data differ according to\r\nthe different measuring principles of sensors (see Fig. 1). Furthermore, nodes in the network\r\ndiffer in their usage profiles [7], adding further complexity (see demand patterns on the left\r\nside in Fig. 1). Despite the high complexity of the task, it is a worthwhile goal to adequately\r\nmeet the challenge of leakage detection and to satisfy the needs of the stakeholders ranging\r\nfrom water utility companies, water management agencies until the general public [8].\r\n1.3. Objectives\r\nThe primary objective is to develop an effective model for detecting leaks in water pipe systems.\r\nThe model should learn the relationships between different system components, including\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 3 ===\r\nsensor types (e.g. flow sensors, pressure sensors, demand measurements, smart meters [5]),\r\nnetwork information (i.e. pipe-attributes corresponding to edge-attributes like diameters, length,\r\nroughness coefficients, as well as geospatial data like coordinates or elevation and physical\r\ninformation), and user patterns (i.e. residential and commercial demand patterns, etc. [7]), to\r\naccurately detect and locate leaks.\r\nExpressed from a more technical perspective, the objective of the model is to predict the\r\nedge-labels for leaky or non-leaky pipes at each time step based on the discrete spatio-temporal\r\ngraph snapshot to localize anomalies. The specific objectives and quantifiable criteria for the\r\nsuccess of the Liquor-HGNN approach are to localize leaks for all pipe sections (corresponding\r\nto edges of a graph) using measurement information from only around 10 percent of the nodes,\r\nto accurately forecast the start time of leaks and to propose an imputation method for handling\r\nmissing data through prior forecasts.\r\n1.4. Method\r\nTo address the complex task of leakage detection, we propose the Liquor-HGNN model1. The\r\nmodel selects the most suitable threshold based the Economic Score Metric, that is built upon\r\nthe metric of KIOS research center [6]. The Economic Score function iterates over the detected\r\nleakages, finds close pipes to each detected leakage, and calculates the score contribution for\r\neach true detection based on the detected distance and the number of true detections. Our\r\nmetric also applies a penalty if a leakage is not detected. The Liquor-HGNN model takes into\r\naccount the physical positions of nodes, their connections via edges as well as the user patterns\r\nwithin the hydraulic demand distribution [7]. It leverages sparsely distributed sensor data and\r\nincorporates a graph attention mechanism to focus on the most relevant parts of the graph.\r\nMethodologically, our approach involves a preprocessing procedure to interpolate demand\r\nvalues for every node. The demand over time is divided into different components, including\r\na global trend, seasonal fluctuations, and weekly and daily variations. These components are\r\nderived from the predictions of the demand measurements and are separated according to their\r\ndemand patterns. The predictions are then multiplied with the steady-state demand or base\r\ndemand of each node [7].\r\nTo handle the heterogeneous graph structure, our model employs heterogeneous message-\r\npassing layers. This enables the model to capture and utilize the diverse sensor types present in\r\nthe water pipe system as different node types in the network. The heterogenity allows to learn\r\nfrom the various types of relationships present in the graph. To introduce different edge-weights\r\nin heterogeneous graph neural networks, we use the HeteroConv wrapper [9]. This is described\r\npartly in the appendix.\r\n2. Related Work\r\nIn this section, we first review the 18 submissions made to the BattLeDIM challenge, which\r\nserve as the main benchmarking results for the used dataset. The first submission is the\r\nensemble multivariate changepoint detection (EMCPD) by Cheng et al. [10]. This method\r\n1https://github.com/MilanShao/Liquor-HGNN/blob/main/README.md\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 4 ===\r\ncombines six algorithms, including changepoint detection, non-parametric multiple changepoint\r\nanalysis, divisive hierarchical estimation, kernel changepoint analysis, and Bayesian estimation\r\nof abrupt changes and trends. Huang et al. [11] propose a method consisting of five stages:\r\nmodel decomposition, data partitioning, nodal demand calibration, calibration residual-based\r\nleakage detection, and an improved vectorial angle method for leakage localization. The\r\nLeakbusters team, represented by Daniel et al. [12], developed a method with two algorithm\r\ncomponents. The first component analyzes pressure differences between pairs of nodes using\r\nSCADA data to identify leakage events. The model is trained on normal time periods, and the\r\nreconstruction error is used to detect leakage events. Saldarriaga et al. [13] utilize a genetic\r\nalgorithm (GA) to identify leaks, while Wang et al. [14] from the Tsinghua Team employ a\r\nmultistage approach involving empirical mode decomposition, extraction of daily and weekly\r\nseasonalities, and emitter representation. The Under Pressure team, represented by Steffelbauer\r\net al. [15], introduces a hierarchical approach for leak diagnosis. It involves demand calibration\r\nusing AMR data and measured flows, mathematical optimization for calibrating pipe roughness,\r\nand the use of a dual network for leak start time detection and localization. Zhang et al. [16]\r\nemploy a fuzzy similarity priority ratio for leakage localization, while Romero et al. [17] from\r\nthe IRI team combine a model-based and data-driven methodology. Their approach determines\r\nthe usage of the two approaches based on the characteristics of the network’s different areas.\r\nMin et al. [18] and Blocher et al. [19] both use clustering algorithms to solve the task, and\r\nLiu et al. [20] employ an LSTM. Dopazo et al. [21] and Tan et al. [22] utilize machine learning\r\napproaches.\r\nThe winning Tongji Team, represented by Li et al. [23], developed the Multiple Leaks Detection\r\nand Isolation Framework (MLDIF). It employs a gradient iteration algorithm with variable steps\r\nto calibrate model parameters for each zone and predicts water consumption using AMR\r\nmeasurements.\r\nWu et al. [24] and Bhowmick et al. [25] both use time series data decomposition, while\r\nMarzola et al. [26] spatially localize anomalies through an enumerative procedure. Barros et\r\nal. [27] introduce a signal processing approach.\r\nNone of these approaches modeled the task using graph neural networks except for Gardars-\r\nson et al. [28], who trained two Chebyshev polynomial kernel Graph Convolutional Networks.\r\nAlthough they stated to gain a better economic score, their results have not been reproducable\r\naccording to the delivered code and have thus been excluded from the conducted experiments.\r\nOn the other hand, there is an increasing number of papers that use Heterogeneous Graph\r\nNeural Networks (HGNN) for link prediction like HetGNN [29] or MTHetGNN [30].\r\n3. Dataset Description\r\nThe BattLeDIM 2020 dataset [6] was created using a real water distribution network in Cyprus,\r\ncovering a pipe length of 42.6 km. It contains information on pipe breaks, water losses, and two\r\nconsumer types: residential and commercial, each with distinct demand patterns. The dataset\r\nconsists of 782 nodes, 33 of them equipped with pressure sensors providing measurements\r\nevery 5 minutes. The network consists of 905 pipe segments of steel pipes with a roughness\r\ncoefficient between 120-140 of approximately 50 meters length, that are used as edges of the\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 5 ===\r\nGraph Neural Network. All measurements for five-minute timesteps for 80 percent of the year\r\n2018 are considered as training data, 20 percent as validation data and the full year of 2019 as test\r\ndataset. Each node has a unique demand pattern for each consumer type, based on the statistical\r\ncharacteristics. Additionally, 82 Automated Metered Readings (AMRs) offer aggregated demand\r\ndata (see Appendix). The dataset includes the physical network structure and coordinates of\r\nthe pipes, making it suitable for water network simulations with the Water Network Tool for\r\nResilience (WNTR), a Python package which supports pressure-driven demand simulations and\r\nleakage modelling [7]. We predict leakages in the ISO 8601 time format YYYY-MM-DD hh:mm,\r\nwhile the predicted location of the leakage is specified by the link ID.\r\n4. Methodology\r\nThe methodology consists of the simulated-based preprocessing model for data interpolation\r\nand the the heterogeneous graph neural network for leakage.\r\n4.1. Data Input and Preprocessing for missing data\r\nOur proposed approach uses a heterogeneous graph neural network (HGNN) [29] to incorporate\r\nnodes with different node-types. As the water in water distribution networks is characterized\r\nby a certain flow direction, we use a directed graph. A heterogeneous graph can be defined\r\nas a tuple 𝐺ℎ= (𝐺, Φ, Ψ), where 𝐺= (𝑉, 𝐸) is a graph object with given nodes 𝑉and edges\r\n𝐸, Φ∶𝑉→𝒜𝑉is a node type mapping function and Ψ∶𝐸→𝒜𝐸is an edge type mapping\r\nfunction. In the preprocessing step, missing demand values are predicted using the Neural\r\nProphet model [31]. We separate the nodes measuring the signals of the whole distribution\r\nnetwork into two sets of nodes, 𝑃⊂𝑉containing all nodes with pressure measurements and\r\nnodes with demand measurements 𝐷⊂𝑉. Accordingly we define a signal at time 𝑡of the\r\npressure sensor of node 𝑣as 𝑥𝑃𝑣(𝑡) and 𝑥𝐷𝑣(𝑡) as the signal of the demand sensor.\r\nNote that demand of a node is dependent of its usage pattern. In this dataset usage is\r\ndistinguished between residential demand and commercial demand which differ in volume and\r\nperiodicity. In our experiment, nodes are either considered commercial or residential.\r\nDue to the sparsity of data, ninety percent of the nodes lack any measurements, however,\r\ntheir usage pattern (commercial or residential) is given. To address the sparsity of the data and\r\nfill in missing demand values, the Neural Prophet model [31] is employed to learn and predict\r\ndemands for each given usage pattern. Neural Prophet decomposes the time series into several\r\ncomponents according to the following equation in order to find the best shaping functions.\r\nFor each pattern type c for commercial and r for residential, we use all nodes 𝑣∈𝐷to learn a\r\nNeural Prophet Model and predict the demand ̂𝑥𝐷c (𝑡) and ̂𝑥𝐷r (𝑡) at a given time 𝑡as:\r\n̂𝑥𝐷c (𝑡) = 𝑇c(𝑡) + 𝑆c(𝑡) + 𝐸c(𝑡) + 𝐹c(𝑡) + 𝐴c(𝑡) + 𝐿c(𝑡)\r\n(1)\r\n̂𝑥𝐷r (𝑡) = 𝑇r(𝑡) + 𝑆r(𝑡) + 𝐸r(𝑡) + 𝐹r(𝑡) + 𝐴r(𝑡) + 𝐿r(𝑡)\r\n(2)\r\nwhere, 𝑇•(𝑡) - Trend at time 𝑡, 𝑆•(𝑡) - Seasonal effects at time 𝑡, 𝐸•(𝑡) - Event and holiday\r\neffects at time 𝑡, 𝐹•(𝑡) - Regression effects at time 𝑡for future-known exogenous variables, 𝐴•(𝑡) -\r\nAuto-regression effects at time 𝑡based on past observations, 𝐿•(𝑡) - Regression effects at time 𝑡\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 6 ===\r\nFigure 2: Results of the predicted values ̂𝑥𝐷\r\n• (𝑡) (blue fit) in comparison to the black doted measured\r\nvalues 𝑥𝐷\r\n• (𝑡) for residential and commercial patterns.\r\nfor lagged observations of exogenous variable for each usage pattern • ∈{c, r}. Neural-Prophet\r\nis a special type of generalized additive models (GAM), that decomposes the time series into the\r\nabove mentioned six types of components. The trend component uses an automatic changepoint\r\ndetection, while seasonality makes use of Fourier term decomposition. For events the automatic\r\ngiven holidays of each country are taken. For the calculation of regression effects a real-valued\r\nregressor is used. The auto-regression effects are modelled by the so called AR-Net, which is a\r\nfully connected neural network [31]. This adds non-linear effects to the additive model. For the\r\nloss function the Huber loss is used, while the learning rate is optimized within a range test.\r\nAdam is used as optimizer.\r\nIn the next step, we adopt the base demand 𝑥base\r\n𝑣\r\ncalculation by Klise et al. [7] and multiply it\r\nwith the predicted value to get overall demand ̃𝑥𝐷𝑣for a node 𝑣:\r\ñ𝑥𝐷𝑣(𝑡) = {𝑥base\r\n𝑣\r\n⋅̂𝑥𝐷c (𝑡)\r\nif 𝑣has commercial pattern\r\n𝑥base\r\n𝑣\r\n⋅̂𝑥𝐷r (𝑡)\r\nif 𝑣has residential pattern\r\n(3)\r\nIn Fig. 2 the fit of the predicted values ̂𝑥𝐷• (𝑡), represented by the blue line, is plotted against\r\nthe actual measured values 𝑥𝐷• (𝑡). We predict the demand values of the entire network to obtain\r\na representative distribution, while the pressure values are still only taken at the nodes with\r\npressure measurements without interpolation.\r\n4.2. Heterogeneous Graph Learning\r\nWe separate between the two node-types 𝒜𝑉= {NP, P}, where nodes of type NP only have the\r\ndemand values (from demand preprocessing or given demands) and nodes of the node-type P\r\nadditionally have the pressure measurements 𝑥𝑃𝑣(𝑡). Thus the feature vector xj(𝑡) for a node 𝑗is\r\ngiven as:\r\nxj(𝑡) =\r\n⎧⎪\r\n⎨⎪\r\n⎩\r\n𝑥𝑃\r\n𝑗(𝑡) || 𝑥𝐷\r\n𝑗(𝑡)\r\nif 𝑗∈𝑃and 𝑗∈𝐷\r\n𝑥𝑃\r\n𝑗(𝑡) || ̃𝑥𝐷\r\n𝑗(𝑡)\r\nif 𝑗∈𝑃and 𝑗∉𝐷\r\ñ𝑥𝐷\r\n𝑗(𝑡)\r\nif 𝑗∉𝑃and 𝑗∉𝐷\r\n𝑥𝐷\r\n𝑗(𝑡)\r\nif 𝑗∉𝑃and 𝑗∈𝐷\r\n(4)\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 7 ===\r\nHere, || denotes the concatenation operator. Additionally we denote four edge types, each\r\nrepresenting a connection between two node types. Thus we have 𝒜𝐸= {(P, P), (NP, NP),\r\n(NP, P), (P, NP)}. We then compute the heterogeneous graph convolution by using the GAT-\r\nConv [32] at a given time 𝑡.\r\nIn the following, we omit the time parameter for brevity as we predict leakages for each point\r\nin time individually.\r\n̂x(e)\r\ni\r\n=\r\n∑\r\n𝑗∈𝒩+\r\n𝑒(𝑖)\r\n𝛼(𝑒)\r\n𝑖,𝑗Θ(𝑒)xj\r\n(5)\r\nHere 𝒩+\r\n𝑒(𝑖) again denotes the neighbors of node 𝑖along edges of type 𝑒∈𝒜𝐸, potentially\r\nincluding the node 𝑖itself, || denotes the concatenation operator and xj is the recorded feature\r\nat node 𝑗. Here Θ(𝑒) is the weight matrix associated with the attention mechanism for each\r\nedge type 𝑒. Pl…4284 tokens truncated…j)\r\n(8)\r\nThe output of the temporal attention layer is presented as:\r\nζt′ =\r\nT\r\nX\r\nt=1\r\nβt′,tZt\r\n(9)\r\n4.3\r\nEncoder Decoder & model training\r\nIn this section, the encoder-decoder structure and whole train-\r\ning process will be summarised. In the encoder stage, Xt ∈\r\nRn is used as the model input. After processed by spatial\r\nattention layer, the middle states are Zt ∈Rm which will\r\nbe used as the input of the decoder. Processed by temporal\r\nattention layer, the final output of the model ˆyt+1 is obtained.\r\nAll the deep neural networks use hyperbolic tangent (tanh)\r\nfunction as the activation function to generate no-linear rep-\r\nresentation defined as follows:\r\ntanh(x) = exp(x) −exp(−x)\r\nexp(x) + exp(−x)\r\n(10)\r\nThe LSTM units are used as the basic elements in the\r\nmodel. LSTM aims at learning a mapping function as fol-\r\nlows:\r\nht, ct = f(Xt, ht−1, st−1)\r\n(11)\r\nwhere Xt is the input at time t, ht and ct are the hidden state\r\nand cell state of LSTM unit at time t-1.\r\nA dropout technique is used to overcome overfitting.\r\nAll the deep neural networks are smooth and differentiable,\r\nwhich makes them trainable via backpropagation algorithm\r\n[Rumelhart et al., 1986]. The mean squared error (MSE) be-\r\ntween the predicted series and the ground truth series is min-\r\nimized by Adam optimizer [Kingma and Ba, 2017] and the\r\nlearning rate is selected accordingly.\r\nAll deep neural networks are implemented in PyTorch\r\nframework.\r\n5\r\nExperiments\r\nIn this section, the details of the dataset used will be pre-\r\nsented first along with the partition of the training set, vali-\r\ndation set and test set. Secondly, a pretreatment process will\r\nbe presented to transform the unstationary series into station-\r\nary series. Then, the evaluation metrics and hyperparame-\r\nter selection will be detailed. After that, the comparison of\r\nthe baseline models and the proposed hDS-RNN will be pre-\r\nsented along with some remarks. Finally, the experiments for\r\na further explanation of the spatial-temporal mechanism em-\r\nployed are conducted and the results are illustrated.\r\n5.1\r\nDatasets\r\nThe experiment was conducted over a real dataset collected\r\nby the water supply company of the city of CZ. Sensors de-\r\nployed in the WDS continuously collected hydraulic param-\r\neters (e.g., flow rate and pressure) for systematic monitoring.\r\nThe dataset ranges from 05/05/2017 to 04/04/2019 with a\r\ntime interval of 30 minutes. The dataset contains time se-\r\nries of 11 flow rate monitoring sensors and 7 pressure mon-\r\nitoring sensors. The 11 flow sensors and 7 pressure sensors\r\nare placed either on the outlet pipes of booster stations or on\r\nwater mains. The flow sensor No.8 (F8) and pressure sensor\r\nNo.5 (P5) are respectively regarded as the target sensors. The\r\ndataset was partitioned into the training set, validation set and\r\ntest set. The three sets are split at 09/01/2018 and 01/01/2019,\r\nwith the scale of approximately 4:1:1.\r\n5.2\r\nPretreatment\r\nThe nonstationarity nature of the input series makes predic-\r\ntion difficult. Nonstationary series can be decomposed into\r\nTrend, Seasonal and Residual [Oliveira et al., 2017]. To\r\nsimplify the forecasting task, the input series is transformed\r\ninto its 1st order derivative to remove Trend.\r\nThen the\r\nderivatived series is additively decomposed into Seasonal\r\nand Residual. The decomposition process is performed only\r\non the training set and the seasonal pattern extracted is ap-\r\nplied to both validation set and test set. The residual series\r\nis the gap between the 1st order derivative series and the sea-\r\nsonal series. Our aim is to predict the residual series to recon-\r\nstruct the original series.\r\n5.3\r\nEvaluation metrics & Hyperparameters\r\nMean squared error (MSE) and mean absolute error (MAE)\r\nare used to evaluate the models. Their effectiveness has been\r\nproved by numerous time-series predicting problems [Liang\r\net al., 2018]. Note that yt ∈Rτ is the ground truth series and\r\nˆyt ∈Rτ is the predicted series. The two criteria are defined\r\nas:\r\nRMSE =\r\nv\r\nu\r\nu\r\nt 1\r\nN\r\nT +τ\r\nX\r\nt=T\r\n(ˆyt −yt)2\r\n(12)\r\nMAE = 1\r\nN\r\nT +τ\r\nX\r\ni=T\r\n(ˆyt −yt)\r\n(13)\r\nSpecifically, because the series is decomposed additively, the\r\nMAE and MSE score applied to either residual series or re-\r\nconstructed series are the same.\r\nDuring the training process, the learning rate for the Adam\r\noptimiser is 0.001. The mini-batch training is employed for\r\nall the deep neural networks. Although small-batch training\r\ncan improve the generalisation performance, it degrades com-\r\nputational parallelism [Masters and Luschi, 2018]. Consider-\r\ning both the training speed and performance, a series of batch\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 5 ===\r\nsizes are tested in a preliminary experiment and a batch size\r\nof 64 is selected.\r\nThe encoder length T is grid searched over {5, 10, 20, 40,\r\n80}. Experiments showed that extending encoder length does\r\nnot improve the performance. As a result, an encoder length\r\nof 60 is selected. Considering the error accumulation and in-\r\nterval length of our dataset, the decoder length is empirically\r\nset to 4 for future prediction. A decoder length of 4 represents\r\n2 hours in the real world and is long enough for short-term\r\noperational decision-making.\r\nFinally, a grid search over {32, 64, 128, 256} is conducted\r\nfor hidden states dimension. Note that the hidden states in the\r\nencoder and decoder are the same. Meanwhile, a grid search\r\nover {1, 2, 4} is conducted for the number of hidden layers.\r\nThe hDA-RNN model which uses 60 as encoder length, 64 as\r\nhidden states dimension and 1 as hidden layer number, out-\r\nperformed the other parameter settings on the validation set.\r\n5.4\r\nModel Comparison\r\nIn this section, the performances of the proposed spatial-\r\ntemporal attention model and the other baseline models are\r\ncompared. The models are compared on the aforementioned\r\ndataset. Flow series (F8) and pressure series (P5) are used as\r\nthe target series.\r\n1. SARIMA [Arandia et al., 2016]:\r\nSeasonal autore-\r\ngressive integrated moving average model (p, d, q) ×\r\n(P, D, Q)s. We use s=48 because the period is 24 hours\r\nand the time interval is 1/2 hour. d = 1 which means 1st\r\norder derivative is used to erase the trend component.\r\nThe other parameters are optimised according to experi-\r\nmental results.\r\n2. SVR [Msiza et al., 2007]: Supported vector regressor, a\r\ntraditional machine learning method widely used in re-\r\ngression tasks.\r\n3. GBRT [Friedman, 2000]: An ensemble of simple mod-\r\nels (regression tree) proven efficient and easy to adapt in\r\nregression tasks.\r\n4. MLP [Koskela et al., 1996]: Multilayer perceptrons\r\n(MLP), the simplest neural networks for time series\r\nproblem.\r\n5. Seq2Seq [Sutskever et al., 2014]: The sequence to se-\r\nquence model is originally used in machine translation.\r\nNow it is slightly modified for multivariate time series\r\nprediction.\r\n6. DA-RNN [Qin et al., 2017]: DS-RNN uses dual-stage\r\nspatial-temporal attention-based RNN in multivariate\r\ntime series prediction. It was the state-of-the-art model\r\nbefore DSTP-RNN.\r\n7. DA-RNN-II: A modified dual-stage spatial-temporal\r\nattention-based RNN model using the spatial attention\r\nmechanism mentioned in Section 4.\r\n8. DSTP-RNN [Liu et al., 2019]: DSTP-RNN uses a dual-\r\nstage two-phase spatial-temporal attention mechanism\r\nand is the state-of-the-art model for multivariate series\r\nprediction.\r\nModels\r\nFLow\r\nPressure\r\nMSE\r\nMAE\r\nMSE\r\nMAE\r\nSARIMA\r\n3.78 E-3\r\n4.18 E-2\r\n1.41 E-2\r\n8.88 E-2\r\nSVR\r\n5.47 E-3\r\n4.89 E-2\r\n1.77 E-2\r\n9.97 E-2\r\nGBRT\r\n5.50 E-3\r\n4.90 E-2\r\n1.72 E-2\r\n9.80 E-2\r\nANN\r\n3.44 E-3\r\n4.25 E-2\r\n1.21 E-2\r\n8.32 E-2\r\nSeq2Seq\r\n3.28 E-3\r\n4.13 E-2\r\n1.27 E-2\r\n8.48 E-2\r\nTransformer\r\n3.09 E-3\r\n4.01 E-2\r\n1.96 E-2\r\n10.4 E-2\r\nDS-RNN\r\n2.17 E-3\r\n3.37 E-2\r\n1.21 E-2\r\n8.30 E-2\r\nDS-RNN-II\r\n2.12 E-3\r\n3.34 E-2\r\n1.23 E-2\r\n8.46 E-2\r\nDSTP-RNN\r\n2.70 E-3\r\n3.72 E-2\r\n1.29 E-2\r\n8.65 E-2\r\nhDS-RNN\r\n2.08 E-3\r\n3.30 E-2\r\n1.17 E-2\r\n8.18 E-2\r\nTable 1: Performance comparison of different models\r\nThe performance of all models tested is shown in Table 1.\r\nIn terms of flow series prediction, the proposed hybrid spatial-\r\ntemporal attention model (hDS-RNN) outperforms all base-\r\nline models. Remarkably, compared with the state-of-the-art\r\nDS-RNN, hDS-RNN model shows 4.1% and 2.1% improve-\r\nments MSE and MAE. DS-RNN-II model uses a modified\r\nspatial attention mechanism and improvements on both cri-\r\nteria could be observed. The hybrid DS-RNN model, which\r\nis a fusion of DS-RNN and DS-RNN-II, achieves the best\r\nperformance among them. However, DSTP-RNN model un-\r\nderperforms the DA-RNN model, which doesn’t accord with\r\nthe result in [Liu et al., 2019]. DSTP-RNN-II model in [Liu\r\net al., 2019] is also tested and no obvious improvement is\r\nobserved. Due to the fact that our dataset is relatively small\r\n(contains only 18 series), the large and complex models do\r\nnot perform well. In the work [Liu et al., 2019], results show\r\nthat the difference of the performance between DA-RNN and\r\nDSTP-RNN is small when the dataset is simple.\r\nIn terms of pressure series prediction, compared with the\r\nstate-of-the-art DA-RNN model, improvements of 1.4% and\r\n3.3% on MSE and MAE are achieved. However, Because of\r\nthe erratic fluctuation of the pressure series, the prediction of\r\npressure faces more difficulties. According to our experimen-\r\ntal results, the improvement achieved by hDS-RNN is small\r\ncompared with traditional time series model SARIMA.\r\n5.5\r\nEvaluation on Temporal attention\r\nThe temporal attention mechanism is employed to capture\r\nlong-term dependency.\r\nIn terms of water flow prediction,\r\nthe Seq2Seq baseline model achieves the best performance\r\nwhen the encoder length is 20. However, the temporal at-\r\ntention based models are capable of capturing longer depen-\r\ndency. Most of them achieve the best performance when en-\r\ncoder length is 60. As presented in Figure 3, the performance\r\ncurves descend steadily as the encoder window extends. Af-\r\nter the optimum point T=60, their performance.s drop rapidly.\r\nSpecifically, when T = 100, DS-RNN model and DS-RNN-\r\nII model don’t converge in the training process. Moreover,\r\nwhen T = 60 and T = 80, DSTP-RNN model fails to\r\nconverge. The results of no-converged experiments are not\r\nshown in Figure 3. Suffering from vanishing gradient[Pas-\r\ncanu et al., 2013], the attention-based model still have diffi-\r\nculty in capturing very long-term dependency. Besides, the\r\nproposed model hDS-RNN outperformed the stat-of-the-art\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 6 ===\r\n(a) Encoder length vs MSE\r\n(b) Encoder length vs MAE\r\nFigure 3: Results of encoder window length vs both metrics in water\r\nflow prediction of 4 main attention-based RNN (DSTP-RNN, DS-\r\nRNN, DS-RNN-II and hDS-RNN).\r\n(a) Decoder length vs MSE\r\n(b) Decoder length vs MAE\r\nFigure 4: Results of decoder window length vs both metrics in water\r\nflow prediction of 4 main attention-based RNN (DSTP-RNN, DS-\r\nRNN, DS-RNN-II and hDS-RNN).\r\nmodel DS-RNN and its modified version DS-RNN-II when\r\nencoder length is 60.\r\n5.6\r\nEvaluation on long-term prediction\r\nInstead of performing the experiment with decoder length\r\nτ = 4, we extend the decoder length from 4 to 10 for long-\r\nterm prediction. The capacities of long-term prediction of\r\nthe four attention-based RNN model (DS-RNN, DS-RNN-II,\r\nDSTP-RNN and hDS-RNN) are compared. Both metrics are\r\nevaluated on different decoder length of the aforementioned\r\nmodels, as shown in Fig 4. We observe that the performances\r\nof the four models are almost of the same level at step 1 and\r\ndiffer at further steps. Remarkably, the proposed hDS-RNN\r\noutperforms the other baseline models at each step. Specif-\r\nically, the performance of DSTP-RNN trends to surpass its\r\ncompetitors in long-term predictions. In [Liu et al., 2019],\r\nexperimental results showed that DSTP-RNN performed bet-\r\nter in long-term prediction. However, in our case, we pay\r\nmore attention on short-term dependency, because in water\r\nflow and pressure series prediction, such long dependency is\r\nnot significant.\r\n5.7\r\nEvaluation on spatial attention of the model\r\nThe spatial distribution of geo-sensors are detailed in Figure\r\n5a. The spatial attention weights are presented in Figure 5b.\r\nIn this experiment, the target series is F8. It can be observed\r\nthat the input series F8 is assigned with high weights. The\r\nweights assigned to flow series F4 is high, the plausible rea-\r\nson is that their physical locations are close and they are con-\r\nnected directly with a long straight pipe. The weights as-\r\nsigned to series F5 and F6 are relatively small compared with\r\nthe other flow series because these two sensors are far away\r\nfrom the target sensor F8. The F1 F2 F3 and P1 P2 P3 are\r\n(a) Sensor location map.\r\n(b) Spatial attention weights.\r\nFigure 5: The map of the sensors’ location and their attention\r\nweights allocated in the model. In this case, ’F8’ is the target sensor.\r\n’F1’, ’F2’, ’P1’, ’P2’ are recorded on the outlet pipe of two remote\r\nbooster stations. ’F3’, ’P3’ are recorded at the nearest booster sta-\r\ntion. The other series are recorded on water mains\r\nthe flow and pressure of outflow from three booster station.\r\nIt can be observed that the spatial attention weights are ap-\r\nproximately proportional to the distance. Flow series F3 and\r\npressure series P3, recorded at the nearest booster station, are\r\nassigned with higher weights.\r\n6\r\nConclusion and future work\r\nIn this paper, we proposed a hybrid dual-stage attention-based\r\nRNN (hDA-RNN) for multivariate time series prediction in\r\nWDS. Following previous work, an encoder-decoder struc-\r\nture with dual-stage spatial-temporal attention mechanism\r\nwas employed. Specifically, we proposed a hybrid spatial at-\r\ntention mechanism in the first stage. In the proposed mech-\r\nanism, the spatial attention weights of a certain sensor at a\r\ncertain time is trained with related inputs along temporal and\r\nspatial axis simultaneously. Additionally, a temporal atten-\r\ntion mechanism is used in the second stage to adaptively al-\r\nlocate different weights for different time steps. Our model is\r\nevaluated on a dataset containing hydraulic monitoring series\r\nin WDS, respectively using a flow series and a pressure series\r\nas the target. Experimental results showed that our model\r\noutperformed 9 baseline models on both metrics. Spatial at-\r\ntention weights are visualised for a further explanation of our\r\napproach.\r\nIn the future, experiments will be conducted on various\r\ndatasets in different fields . In addition, a real-world appli-\r\ncation of our model will be conducted.\r\n7\r\nAcknowledgements\r\nWe would like to thank Dr. Smith for her devoted work in\r\npolishing the language. Our work was jointly supported by\r\nthe National Natural Science Foundation of China (Grant No.\r\n51879139).\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 7 ===\r\nReferences\r\n[Arandia et al., 2016] Ernesto\r\nArandia,\r\nAmadou\r\nBa,\r\nBradley Eck, and Sean McKenna.\r\nTailoring Seasonal\r\nTime Series Models to Forecast Short-Term Water\r\nDemand.\r\nJournal of Water Resources Planning and\r\nManagement, 142(3):04015067, March 2016.\r\n[Bahdanau et al., 2014] Dzmitry\r\nBahdanau,\r\nKyunghyun\r\nCho, and Yoshua Bengio.\r\nNeural machine transla-\r\ntion by jointly learning to align and translate.\r\nCoRR,\r\nabs/1409.0473, 2014.\r\n[Friedman, 2000] Jerome Friedman.   {
    "type": "input_text",
    "text": "Warning: truncated output (original token count: 15199)\nTotal output lines: 764\n\nExplainable graph neural network for real-time demand states forecasting in water distribution system - ScienceDirect (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S259012302502969X)\nciteturn26search0 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 2 months ago; While integrating time series prediction algorithms with their methods could achieve future state forecasting, the simple stacking of time series forecasting and state estimation would lead to error accumulation. ... This study proposes an Attentive Graph Network (AGN) to forecast water demand in distribution systems using pressure data without hydraulic modeling. ... This study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic model support.\n\nResearch paper\n\nExplainable graph neural network for real-time demand states forecasting in water distribution system\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911Get rights and content\n\nUnder a Creative Commons license\n\nOpen access\n\n## Highlights\n\n  * •\n\nAttention-enhanced GNN for real-time demand forecasting based on pressure data.\n\n  * •\n\nModel is independent from hydraulic model, offering broader applicability.\n\n  * •\n\nModel exhibits high spatiotemporal accuracy and sustained long-term effectiveness.\n\n  * •\n\nModel explanation reveals its operational mechanisms, facilitating field deployment.\n\n## Abstract\n\nEfficient real-time nodal demand forecasting in water distribution systems is essential for a reliable and resilient water supply. Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.\n\n## Graphical abstract\n\nImage: Image, graphical abstract\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (273KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\n  * Previous article in issue\n  * Next article in issue\n\n## Keywords\n\nExplainability\n\nReal-time\n\nDemand forecasting\n\nAttention mechanisms\n\nGraph neural network\n\nWater distribution system\n\n## 1. Introduction\n\nAs urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [10,25]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [11,35]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. The stochastic variability of water usage, driven by human activities, complicates the forecasting process [26]. Given the hurdles in distribution reliability and the capricious nature of water consumption, accurate real-time demand forecasting is crucial for ensuring a reliable and resilient WDS [12].\n\nGenerally, to estimate the water usage at non-monitored sites, a well-calibrated hydraulic model is typically required for indirect observation [4,5,9,15]. The hydraulic model, with their robust theoretical basis and comprehensible parameter settings, serves as the foundation and primary source of information for water network management [8,27,36], and is widely understood and trusted by practitioners. Hydraulic simulation was conducted to enhance the estimation credibility of a real-world network, and demand forecasting can then be obtained by time series forecasting algorithms [23]. However, the uncertainties associated with the water demand and the condition of infrastructure assets make constructing and maintaining a well-calibrated WDS highly complex and time-consuming [1,33]. Achieving real-time efficient forecasting based on the hydraulic model seems unattainable, especially for cities lacking a hydraulic model. Consequently, the primary objective of this study focuses on developing an innovative state estimation framework that operates independently of conventional hydraulic modeling paradigms.\n\nSeveral studies [18,33,37] have enabled direct modeling of WDSs, obviating the need for hydraulic models. Based on the assumption of smooth variations in nodal head (the sum of nodal pressure and elevation of a node), Zhou et al. [37] developed a graph-based head reconstruction (GHR) method. This method decomposes the hydraulic head into components with different variation speeds, reconstructing the head using the slowly varying components obtained from a limited number of monitoring nodes. However, this method heavily relies on having a sufficient number of well-defined sensor nodes and a regular underlying graph structure. Xing and Sela [33] were the pioneers in employing a graph neural network (GNN) to achieve state estimation within WDSs. They developed an encoder-decoder GNN architecture, enabling real-time synchronous estimation of pipeline flow and nodal pressure states. Their research demonstrated the robustness of the GNN model to various levels of noise. By applying the gated graph neural network (GGNN), Li et al. [18] were the first to achieve the state forecasting for water quality. The GGNN algorithm, derived from gated recurrent unit (GRU), was applied for the first time in the domain of the WDS, yielding promising spatiotemporal predictive outputs. Although the aforementioned methods can be used for predicting future water demand states, they still have limitations. The methods in the first two studies were limited to estimating the current state. While integrating time series prediction algorithms with their methods could achieve future state forecasting, the simple stacking of time series forecasting and state estimation would lead to error accumulation. The GGNN method offers a novel approach to developing alternative models for state forecasting in WDSs. However, directly applying the GGNN method does not tightly integrate with the characteristics of water networks. Additionally, GGNN requires recurrent graph convolution operations to expand the receptive field, which limits its application in large-scale pipe networks and leads to over-smoothing issues.\n\nDespite excellent results and a broad range of applications, GNN models are still treated as black boxes [16]: the underlying mechanisms that steer the learning process and generalization ability remain a challenging and open question [29]. Merely achieving good performance is not enough to guarantee the deployment of models in production [13,21], especially in critical operational contexts where additional performance metrics such as robustness or safety are paramount. In such environments, human operators must comprehend and trust the model's predictions. Also, a deeper understanding of the model would improve its predictive performance [22]. Therefore, the second goal of this paper is to explain the results generated by the model.\n\nThis study innovative proposes an explainable attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) framework for water demand states forecasting from a pressure perspective. Derived from GRU-based GNN (i.e. GGNN), this model not only exhibits excellent time series prediction performance but also captures the network’s structure. The innovative attention mechanisms are then incorporated to address the limitation of GGNN in capturing solely local dependencies and enhance the model's interpretability through its transparent decision-making process. Overall, the paper encompasses the following novelties:\n\n·Demand forecasting model designed for water network characteristics: A core innovation of this study is the development of the AGN model, specifically designed to capture the unique spatio-temporal characteristics of water distribution networks. The proposed AGN model forecasts future demands at all nodes, without relying on hydraulic models. The AGN model shows significant accuracy in both spatial and temporal dimensions compared to existing direct modeling methods. The model maintains high predictive robustness across various forecasting horizons. Our high accuracy results validate the feasibility and efficiency of demand prediction from a pressure perspective.\n\nModel explanation associated with water network characteristics: Merely achieving sufficient prediction accuracy is not enough to guarantee the model’s deployment in real-world WDSs. To bridge this gap, the second innovation addresses the crucial need for improved model transparency. Specifically, the key lies in integrating model explanations with practical engineering issues, enabling network managers to understand and trust the model, thereby making reliable decisions. To the best knowledge, we are the first to address interpretability issues in WDS. By providing these explanations, this study provided a deeper understanding of the roles of different model components in the prediction task. This process also aids in improving the model by offering feedback to further enhance its predictive performance.\n\n## 2. Methodology\n\nThis study proposes an Attentive Graph Network (AGN) to forecast water demand in distribution systems using pressure data without hydraulic modeling. The architecture leverages the inherent graph structure of water networks, historical pressure measurements, and nodal features to predict future nodal demands. Specifically designed to capture unique pressure characteristics in water distribution systems (WDS), the model includes built-in interpretability mechanisms to ensure transparency. The following sections detail the challenges addressed and present the explainable AGN framework.\n\n### 2.1. Problem formulation\n\nBefore discussing the issues and challenges, this study provide a detailed introduction to the symbolic representation of WDS as a graph. The WDS is represented as an undirected graph \\mathcal{G}=\\left(\\mathcal{V},\\mathcal{E},\\mathcal{W}\\right). Here, \\mathcal{V} is the set of I+{I}^{*} vertices representing nodes that comprise reservoirs, tanks and junctions. \\mathcal{E} is the set of edges representing the valves, pumps, and pipes. \\mathcal{W}\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{\\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)\\times \\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)} is the weighted adjacency matrix. Given the nodal pressures {p}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}}=\\left[\\begin{array}{ccc}{p}_{{v}_{1},{t}_{1}}& \\cdots & {p}_{{v}_{1},{t}_{J}}\\\\ ⋮& \\ddots & ⋮\\\\ {p}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{1}}& \\cdots & {p}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}}\\end{array}\\right]\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{I\\times J} at I vertices of historical J time steps, nodal heights F{h}_{{v}_{I}}\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{I} and nodal types F{t}_{{v}_{I}}\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{I} at I vertices, the training task is to forecast next {J}^{*} time steps demand, denoted as {h}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J*}}=\\left[\\begin{array}{ccc}{h}_{{v}_{1},{t}_{1}}& \\cdots & {h}_{{v}_{1},{t}_{J*}}\\\\ ⋮& \\ddots & ⋮\\\\ {h}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{1}}& \\cdots & {h}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J*}}\\end{array}\\right]\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{I\\times {J}^{*}}, that achieves trustworthy approximations of the ground truths {\\widehat{h}}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J*}}, which is given by:(3)Training\\left({p}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}}+F{h}_{{v}_{I}}+F{t}_{{v}_{I}}\\right)=\\underset{\\Theta }{argmin}\\frac{1}{{J}^{*}}\\sum _{J}^{J+{J}^{*}}\\left|{h}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J*}}-{\\widehat{h}}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J*}}\\right|where \\Theta denotes all learnable parameters in the AGN model. It is crucial to emphasize that assuming inputs from pressure sensors and calculating losses based on all nodes is impractical in real situations. That is, nodes to be generalized (i.e. non-sensor nodes) cannot be considered known during training to prevent information leakage [19,33]. Thus, despite the aim of this study is to achieve prediction of all nodes, the input dimension of {h}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}} in Eq. (3) is I\\times J, instead of \\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)\\times J.\n\nThe training challenge lies in learning the transferable correlation between sensor measurements and nodal features. After acquiring the trained model of the I sensor nodes, forecasting all I+{I}^{*} nodes are accomplished by additionally inputting the F{h}_{{v}_{{I}^{*}}} and F{t}_{{v}_{{I}^{*}}} of the remaining {I}^{*} non-sensor nodes:(4)Generalizing\\left({p}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}}+F{h}_{{v}_{I+{I}^{*}}}+F{t}_{{v}_{I+{I}^{*}}}\\right)={f}_{\\Theta }\\left({p}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}}+F{h}_{{v}_{I+{I}^{*}}}+F{t}_{{v}_{I+{I}^{*}}}\\right)={h}_{{v}_{I+I*},{t}_{J*}}\n\n### 2.2. Explainable forecasting framework\n\nThe GRU [6] is a highly optimized variant of the recurrent neural network. Its core advantage lies in its unique architectural design. Through the precise regulatory mechanisms of the update gate and reset gate, the GRU skillfully balances the retention and forgetting of historical information [7]. This allows it to capture the dynamics of long-term and short-term dependencies in time series data [24]. The GGNN, an innovative extension of the GRU [17], integrates recurrent layers, graph convolution, and gating mechanisms. It retains the advantages of the GRU in time series prediction while deeply analyzing the complex structure of non-Euclidean space graph data through graph convolution operations, achieving efficient utilization of graph data [31]. However, the GGNN also faces limitations of the convolution-based graph methods: Each iteration only focuses on the first-order neighborhood of the sensor nodes. Thus, the iteration of the recurrent layers restricts the generalization of sensor node features to a larger network space [32].\n\nBuilding upon the dynamic global weighting capability of attention mechanisms, this study enhances the GGNN framework by integrating self-attention and external-attention modules. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. Fig. 1 illustrates the overall AGN architecture. The subsequent sections detail the design and implementation of this framework:\n\nImage: Fig 1\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (318KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 1. The workflow of the proposed AGN model.\n\n(1) Neighbor propagation based on adjacency matrix: By multiplying candidate hidden pressure states (CHPS) {c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)} with the weighted adjacency matrix {\\mathcal{W}}_{{v}_{i}}, the past CHPS of sensor node {v}_{i} is transferred to its first-order neighborhood nodes:((5)){\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}={\\mathcal{W}}_{{v}_{i}}·{c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}where l is the iteration of recurrent layers, and the initial CHPS {c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(0\\right)} is an initialized learnable parameter.\n\n(2) Long-short-term memory augmented by self-attention mechanism: By calculating the matrix product of the query vector {Q}_{·}(·) and the key vector {K}_{·}(·) of the node sequence (Eqs. (6)-a and 7-a), the attention between node sequences can be reflected. The attention scores are obtained after normalization through SoftMax activation (Eqs. (6)-b and 7-b). The value vectors {V}_{·}(·) of all nodes in the sequence are weighted and summed using the attention scores. After passing through the sigmoid activation \\sigma (·), the retention weights of short-term memory {r}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)} and long-term memory {u}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)} can be obtained ((6), (7)):(6){r}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}=\\sigma \\left({\\stackrel{‾}{Ar}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}·{V}_{r}\\left({p}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\parallel {\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right)\\right)(6-a)A{r}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}=\\frac{{Q}_{r}\\left({p}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\parallel {\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right)·{K}_{r}\\left({p}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\parallel {\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right)}{\\sqrt{D}}(6-b){\\stackrel{‾}{Ar}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}=\\frac{\\mathrm{exp}\\left(A{r}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right)}{{\\sum }_{{v}_{i}\\in \\mathcal{V}}exp\\left(A{r}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right)}(7){u}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}=\\sigma \\left({\\stackrel{‾}{Au}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}·{V}_{u}\\left({p}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\parallel {\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right)\\right)(7-a)A{u}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}=\\frac{{Q}_{u}\\left({p}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\parallel {\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right),{K}_{u}\\left({p}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\parallel {\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right)}{\\sqrt{D}}(7-b){\\stackrel{‾}{Au}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}=\\frac{\\mathrm{exp}\\left(A{u}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right)}{{\\sum }_{{v}_{i}\\in \\mathcal{V}}exp\\left(A{u}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right)}where {\\stackrel{‾}{Ar}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)} and {\\stackrel{‾}{Au}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)} are the normalized attention scores of the reset gate and update gate, respectively. {Q}_{·}(·), {K}_{·}(·), and {V}_{·}(·) are three different linear transformations. The symbol \\parallel denotes the concatenation operation of pressure measurement {p}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)} and past CHPS {\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}. D is the dimension of the concatenation, preventing gradient explosion.\n\n## 4. Results and discussion\n\nThis section presents the results from both forecasting performance and model explainability analyses. First, this study report and analyze the forecasting task results: the proposed method achieves superior spatial generalization and temporal forecasting accuracy compared to previous approaches. Subsequently, this study evaluates the robustness of the AGN model across varying prediction windows. Finally, once demonstrated the quality of our predictions, this study explains the recurrent layers, attention mechanisms and nodal features used in the model, demonstrating how these components influence the final output of the model.\n\n### 4.1. Forecasting results\n\n#### 4.1.1. Spatial generalization accuracy\n\nThis study aims to utilize the GNN algorithm to predict the water demand states from a pressure perspective. A thorough review of previous researc…5199 tokens truncated… we proposed hy-\r\nbrid spatial-temporal attention-based RNN (hDA-RNN) for\r\nthe modelling of multivariate time series prediction. The con-\r\ntributions of our work are as follows:\r\n1. To our best knowledge, this work firstly uses recurrent\r\nnetworks with attention mechanism to capture spatial-\r\ntemporal correlations of flow and pressure series in\r\nWDS for their short-term forecasting.\r\n2. A hybrid spatial attention mechanism is proposed to\r\nenhance the spatial correlation learning in the encoder\r\nstage.\r\nThe proposed method uses inputs along both\r\ntemporal and spatial axes for the training of attention\r\nweighs. Promising results are achieved in the experi-\r\nments.\r\n3. Experiments are conducted on a real-world dataset.\r\nThe proposed model hDA-RNN outperformed 9 base-\r\nline models, including the traditional time series model\r\nSARIMA and the state-of-the-art attention-based RNN.\r\n2\r\nRelated Work\r\nOur work focuses on two tasks: the forecasting of flow and\r\npressure series in WDS. Short-term water demand forecasting\r\nis comprehensively investigated. Because the short-term wa-\r\nter demand exhibits very similar pattern to the flow series that\r\nwe discussed, there are numerous models that we can refer to.\r\nFor short-term water demand forecasting, both traditional\r\nmodels and deep networks are widely investigated. [Aran-\r\ndia et al., 2016] used seasonal autoregressive integrated mov-\r\ning average model (SARIMA). SARIMA is easy to adapt and\r\nrequires small training set (7 days) to achieve promising re-\r\nsults. Furthermore, using deep learning methods, [Guo et al.,\r\n2018] proposed a deep networks which leverages GRU lay-\r\ners and dense layers and outperformed SARIMA. [Msiza et\r\nal., 2007] compared the traditional machine learning model:\r\nsupported vector regressor (SVR) and the multilayer percep-\r\ntrons (MLP). A variety of the kernels types of SVR has been\r\ntested and promising results were reached.\r\nHowever, the\r\nSVR model still underperformed MLp which employs deeper\r\nstructure and more parameters to extract complex no-linear\r\nrepresentation of the inputs[Msiza et al., 2007]. The fore-\r\ncasting of pressure series which exhibits no obvious regular-\r\nity faces more difficulties. Efficient models need to be devel-\r\noped.\r\nRecent advancement of attention mechanism has brought\r\nsignificant improvement in various field. Inspired by the hu-\r\nman attention mechanism, the attention-based RNN model\r\nwas first proposed in a machine translation task [Bahdanau\r\net al., 2014].\r\nEmploying the encoder-decoder structure\r\nand the attention mechanism applied to the encoding se-\r\nquence, the model became, at that time, the start-of-the-\r\nart work in English-to-French translation. Later, the well-\r\nknown Transformer [Vaswani et al., 2017] dispensed with\r\nthe recurrent module and solely employed several Multi-\r\nHead attention modules and feed-forward modules. Proven\r\nefficient in sequence to sequence tasks like natural lan-\r\nguage process [Bahdanau et al., 2014; Vaswani et al., 2017;\r\nYang et al., 2016] and computer vision tasks [Xu et al., 2015;\r\nHuang et al., 2019], the attention-based models were also\r\nwidely used in time series problems. Dual-stage attention-\r\nbased RNN (DS-RNN) [Qin et al., 2017] employed two atten-\r\ntion modules for spatial-temporal correlation capturing and\r\noutperformed the other traditional nonlinear autoregressive\r\nexogenous models. GeoMAN [Liang et al., 2018] used three-\r\nstage attention respectively applied to local features, global\r\nfeatures and temporal sequence for geo-sensory series pre-\r\ndiction. To enhance the correlation of the target series and\r\nexogenous series, a dual-stage two-phase attention model,\r\nnamely DSTP-RNN [Liu et al., 2019], was proposed. The\r\nfirst-phase spatial attention was applied to the original series.\r\nThe second-phase spatial attention was applied to the con-\r\ncatenation of the former output and the target series. The\r\nsecond stage (temporal attention) was the same as the afore-\r\nmentioned works.\r\nHowever, among the current attention-based RNN models,\r\none essential bottleneck could be the information loss during\r\nthe training of attention weights. Specifically, the spatial at-\r\ntention mechanism only considers inputs along the temporal\r\naxis and ignores exogenous inputs. Hence, we proposed a\r\nhybrid spatial attention mechanism in the encoder stage. It\r\ncould simultaneously capture inputs along temporal and spa-\r\ntial axes to training the attention weights. To the best of our\r\nknowledge, no prior work has used a similar method in the\r\nspatial attention stage.\r\n3\r\nNotation & Problem statement\r\nGiven n ∈N+ series over a window length of T ∈N+ as\r\ninputs, we use xk\r\nt ∈R to represent the reading of k-th sensor\r\nat time t. We use Xt = (x1\r\nt, x2\r\nt, .., xn\r\nt )T ∈Rn×T to represent\r\nthe encoder input series of all geo-sensors at time t. In addi-\r\ntion, the output of the encoder layer is represented by Zt =\r\n(z1\r\nt , z2\r\nt , .., zm\r\nt )T ∈Rm×T , where m is the number of hidden\r\nstates. The reading of the target sensor at time tis represented\r\nby yt. Given τ as the window length of predicted series, the\r\ntarget series for prediction which ranges from T to T + τ can\r\nbe represented by Y = (yT +1, yT +2, ..., yT +τ)T ∈Rτ. The\r\nhistorical part of target series y1\r\nt , y2\r\nt , .., yn\r\nt is included in in-\r\nput series. We use ˆY = (ˆyT +1, ˆyT +2, ..., ˆyT +τ)T ∈Rτ to\r\nrepresent the predicted series.\r\nA mapping from historical multivariate series to the future\r\ntarget series is expected to be learned. Note F(·) as the map-\r\nping function of the model, the problem is defined as follows:\r\nˆyT +1, ˆyT +2, ..., ˆyT +τ = F(x1\r\nt, x2\r\nt, .., xn\r\nt )\r\n(1)\r\n4\r\nMethods\r\nThe overall framework of proposed hybrid DS-RNN is pre-\r\nsented in Figure 2. The encoder-decoder structure [Sutskever\r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 3 ===\r\nFigure 2: The framework of the proposed hybrid dual-stage at-\r\ntention networks (hDA-RNN). In the first stage, the spatial at-\r\ntention weights ak\r\nt is trained with the inputs along the tempo-\r\nral axis (xk\r\n1, xk\r\n2, .., xk\r\nT )T ∈Rn and those along the spatial axis\r\n(x1\r\nt, x2\r\nt, .., xn\r\nT )T ∈Rn. In addition, the hidden states and cell states\r\nof the previous LSTM unit he,t−1, se,t−1 are employed to train the\r\nspatial attention weights. The output of the encoder is represented as\r\nZt = (z1\r\nt , z2\r\nt , .., zm\r\nt )T ∈Rm×T . The output of the decoder is represented as\r\n(ˆyT +1, ˆyT +2, ..., ˆyT +τ)T ∈Rτ\r\net al., 2014], proven efficient in sequence to sequence mod-\r\nelling, is employed in our framework.\r\nThe LSTM unit\r\n[Hochreiter and Schmidhuber, 1997] is used as the basic unit\r\nin both encoder and decoder. A dual-stage attention mech-\r\nanism [Qin et al., 2017] i.e., a spatial attention layer in en-\r\ncoder and a temporal attention layer in decoder, is employed.\r\nSpecifically, a hybrid spatial attention mechanism is proposed\r\nin the first stage. In the second stage, the temporal attention\r\nmechanism is employed.\r\n4.1\r\nSpatial attention in encoder\r\nThe spatial attention mechanism is performed in the first\r\nstage.\r\nFollowing previous work, a dual-stage attention-\r\nbased RNN (DA-RNN)[Qin et al., 2017] serves as an im-\r\nportant baseline in our experiment. {\"in_channels\": (-1, -1), \"out_channels\": out_channels, \"add_self_loops\": False}\r\n        elif args.layer == \"SAGEConv\":\r\n            layer = SAGEConv\r\n            kwargs = False}\r\n        elif args.layer == \"GraphConv\":\r\n            layer = GraphConv\r\n            kwargs = {\"in_channels\": (-1, -1), \"out_channels\": hidden_channels, \"add_self_loops\": False}\r\n            last_kwargs = {\"in_channels\": (-1, -1), \"out_channels\": out_channels, \"add_self_loops\": False}\r\n        elif args.layer == \"LEConv\":\r\n            layer = LEConv\r\n            kwargs = {\"in_channels\": (-1, -1), \"out_channels\": hidden_channels, \"add_self_loops\": False}\r\n            last_kwargs = {\"in_channels\": (-1, -1), \"out_channels\": out_channels, \"add_self_loops\": False}\r\n        else:\r\n            raise NotImplementedError(f\"Layer {args.layer} is not implemented\")\r\n\r\n        if args.num_layer <= 1:\r\n            self.conv_layers = nn.ModuleList([layer(**last_kwargs)])\r\n            self.lin_layers = nn.ModuleList([Linear(-1, out_channels)])\r\n        else:\r\n            self.conv_layers = nn.ModuleList([layer(**kwargs)])\r\n            self.lin_layers = nn.ModuleList([Linear(-1, hidden_channels)])\r\n            for i in range(args.num_layer - 2):\r\n                self.conv_layers.append(layer(**kwargs))\r\n                self.lin_layers.append(Linear(-1, hidden_channels))\r\n\r\n            self.conv_layers.append(layer(**last_kwargs))\r\n            self.lin_layers.append(Linear(-1, out_channels))\r\n\r\n    def forward(self, x, edge_index, edge_attr, edge_weight):\r\n        kwargs = {\"edge_index\": edge_index}\r\n        if self.args.layer == \"GATConv\":\r\n            kwargs[\"edge_attr\"] = edge_attr\r\n        elif self.args.layer == \"LEConv\":\r\n            kwargs[\"edge_weight\"] = edge_weight\r\n\r\n        for conv, lin in zip(self.conv_layers[:-1], self.lin_layers[:-1]):\r\n            x = conv(x, **kwargs) + lin(x)\r\n            x = x.relu()\r\n        x = self.conv_layers[-1](x, **kwargs) + self.lin_layers[-1](x)\r\n        return x\r\n\r\n\r\nclass EdgeDecoder(torch.nn.Module):\r\n    def __init__(self, hidden_channels):\r\n        super().__init__()\r\n        self.lin1 = Linear(2 * hidden_channels, hidden_channels)\r\n        self.lin2 = Linear(hidden_channels, 1)\r\n\r\n    def forward(\r\n            self,\r\n            z_dict,\r\n            # ptp_edge_label_index,\r\n            ptnp_edge_label_index,\r\n            nptp_edge_label_index,\r\n            nptnp_edge_label_index,\r\n    ):\r\n        # row, col = edge_label_index\r\n        # z = torch.cat(\r\n            [\r\n                z_dict[\"pnode\"][ptnp_edge_label_index[0]],\r\n                z_dict[\"onode\"][ptnp_edge_label_index[1]],\r\n            ],\r\n            dim=-1,\r\n        )\r\n        z_nptp = torch.cat(\r\n            [\r\n                z_dict[\"onode\"][nptp_edge_label_index[0]],\r\n                z_dict[\"pnode\"][nptp_edge_label_index[1]],\r\n self.lin1(z).relu()\r\n        z = self.lin2(z)\r\n        return z.view(-1)\r\n\r\n\r\nclass LiquorGNNModel(torch.nn.Module):\r\n    def __init__(self, metadata, args):\r\n        super().__init__()\r\n        self.encoder = GNNEncoder(args.hidden_channels, args.hidden_channels, args)\r\n        self.encoder = to_hetero(self.encoder, metadata, aggr=\"sum\")\r\n        self.decoder = EdgeDecoder(args.hidden_channels)\r\n\r\n    def forward(\r\n            self,\r\n            x_dict,\r\n            edge_index_dict,\r\n            # ptp_edge_label_index,\r\n            ptnp_edge_label_index,\r\n"

Finally, the detection of a single\r\nnew leak occurring in the WDN, along with its starting time, may\r\nbe accomplished by applying change point detection (Step LI-3) to\r\nthe time series kMREsðtÞkF. After a leak is definitively identified\r\nand becomes distinct, the model may be recalibrated to be reset for\r\nnew leakage identification.\r\nMultiple simultaneous leaks will also be reflected in the time\r\nseries kMREsðtÞkF in an aggregated form [compare Eq. Thus, for a more pre-\r\ncise analysis of each individual leak within the overall aggregated\r\nerror, we manually selected, for each leak, an individual combina-\r\ntion of nodes that revealed the leak signal in the most distinct\r\nfashion.\r\nLI-3: Change Point Detection\r\nThe last task of leakage identification is the detection of variations\r\nin the MRE time series induced by a leak flow; therefore, this rep-\r\nresents a change point detection problem. Change point detection\r\nproblems have been widely studied in the literature, and there is a\r\nmultitude of statistical methods available. For a comprehensive\r\nreview we refer the reader to Aminikhanghahi and Cook (2017).\r\nAs explained in the previous section, we expect the MRE to be\r\nunbiased and normally distributed for the network state without\r\nleaks. With the start of a leak event, the error mean will either\r\nabruptly or gradually shift away from zero, depending on the nature\r\nof the leak. Successful change point detection aims to flag these\r\ninstances as early as possible, while at the same time avoiding false\r\nalarms. In LILA, we apply the widely used Cusum algorithm\r\n(Montgomery 2020), known from statistical process monitoring,\r\nto automatically detect changes in the MRE.\r\nThe Cusum algorithm is a method to detect changes in the prob-\r\nability distribution of an univariate signal. It accumulates the dis-\r\ntance Ci of the target variable x from its mean μx, corrected by a\r\nslack value K, which is chosen based on the change of mean that is\r\nto be detected. Often K is selected based on the estimated standard\r\ndeviation σ\u0003x of the target variable multiplied by a parameter δ\r\n[cf. Eq. (16)]. A change point is flagged once Ci exceeds a prede-\r\nfined threshold H ¼ η × σx. In general, the algorithm comes in two\r\nforms for detecting positive and negative changes from the mean.\r\nFor our application, we use the formulation to detect changes in the\r\npositive direction because leaks will manifest themselves as posi-\r\ntive values in the MRE (cf. section LI-2: Reconstruction Error\r\nAnalysis)\r\nCþ\r\ni ¼ max½0; xi −ðμ0 þ KÞ þ Cþ\r\ni−1\u0002\r\nwith\r\nCþ\r\n0 ¼ 0\r\nand\r\nK ¼ δ\r\n2 · σ\u0003x\r\nð16Þ\r\nTo evaluate the general suitability of the Cusum method for the\r\nleak identification problem, we created artificial error trajectories\r\nby adding different levels of white noise to given ground truth leak\r\ntrajectories. This allowed us to confirm the algorithm’s ability to\r\nrobustly flag the leaks and to select the hyperparameters in a rea-\r\nsonable range (cf. Appendix). Moreover, we can derive some useful\r\nintuitions. Naturally, the change point method performs better for\r\nhigher ratios of leak magnitude to signal noise. This reflects the fact\r\nthat a smaller model error and better signal quality enable earlier\r\nleak detection. The same holds for the temporal development of the\r\nleak, as abrupt bursts are easier to detect than slow transients.\r\nLeakage Localization\r\nThe leakage localization step has three subcomponents, including\r\nwater demand calibration, pressure difference analysis to identify\r\nthe pipes with the highest influence on the pressure sensors, and the\r\napplication of the SLA method developed by Berglund et al. (2017)\r\nto quantify the approximate leakage magnitude and localize the\r\nleak in the WDN. The three steps shown in Fig. 1 work sequentially\r\nto, first, partially calibrate the hydraulic model using demand data.\r\nSecond, a pressure difference analysis is conducted to define the\r\norder of the pipes that affect each pressure sensor of the water net-\r\nwork and report them as leak candidates. Third, the application of\r\nSLA aims at finding the magnitude of a leak from the candidates\r\nthat could be producing the discrepancy between nonleak and leak\r\npressure values. A detailed explanation of each leak localization\r\ncomponent is presented in what follows.\r\nLL-1: Water Demand Calibration\r\nWater demand calibration aims at matching the simulated and the\r\nobserved demand to replicate real conditions with the hydraulic\r\nmodel (Walski 2017). LILA requires a demand-calibrated model\r\nto replicate the demand and the pressure measured values reported\r\nby the sensors. To calibrate the demand patterns and base demand\r\nin the WDN hydraulic model, we use automatic meter reading\r\n(AMR) data available for the simulation period. The new calibrated\r\n© ASCE\r\n04022025-4\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022025\r\nDownloaded from ascelibrary.org by Andrea Cominola on 03/30/22. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 5\r\ndemand patterns do not distinguish the type of user, e.g., residential\r\nor commercial, and overwrite the original demand patterns of the\r\nnetwork with the new patterns obtained by normalizing the con-\r\nsumption reported by the AMR data, as shown in Eq.
