The pressure difference analysis evaluates the\r\neffect on sensors of a fixed-size leak inserted at each pipe of the\r\nnetwork by comparing pressure values with and without the leak.\r\nBased on the anomalies between the leak and nonleak nodal pres-\r\nsure values modeled with hydraulic simulation, in Step LL-2 we\r\ndefine a pressure difference matrix (PDM), where each element\r\nei;j is calculated as follows:\r\nei;j ¼\r\nX\r\nNTS\r\nt¼1\r\npt\r\nleaki;j −pt\r\nnoLeaki;j\r\nð19Þ\r\nwhere ei;j represents the cumulative error between pressure with\r\n(pleak) and without a leak (pnoLeak) from the sensor located at node\r\ni after a fixed-size leak is inserted on pipe j; pleak and pnoLeak are\r\nobtained with hydraulic simulation, implemented in LILA using\r\nthe Water Network Tool for Resilience (WNTR) Python package\r\n(Klise et al. 2018); and NTS is the number of hydraulic time steps of\r\nthe hydraulic model. The fixed leak used in this study corresponds\r\nto a flow of approximately 5% of the network’s average flow, based\r\non assumptions made in previously conducted research (Berglund\r\net al. 2017; Kabaasha et al. 2020). This fixed-size leak corresponds\r\nto a flow rate of a background leak in the water network. The PDM\r\nis calculated once for the entire process, and its purpose is only to\r\nselect the pipes affecting the most for each pressure sensor regard-\r\nless of the number of simultaneous leaks. In the case of a significant\r\nmismatch between the estimated and the actual leak size, Step LL-3\r\ndescribed in what follows updates the final estimated leak magni-\r\ntude. The simulation duration corresponds to the extended period\r\nof simulation (EPS) of the WDN hydraulic model, and pressure\r\nvalues are sampled at the end of the EPS to account for seasonality\r\neffects. The dimensions of the PDM are the number of pressure\r\nsensors as rows and the number of network pipes as columns.\r\nTo identify the pipes that produce the highest effect on each pres-\r\nsure sensor after the fixed leak is inserted, we sort each row of PDM\r\nin descending order. We then include these pipes as candidate pipes\r\nfor mathematical programming optimization to find the true leak\r\nlocation, described in the following section.\r\nLL-3: Successive Linear Approximation\r\nIn the SLA step we formulate a simulation-optimization problem.\r\nThe objective function, which is minimized, represents the absolute\r\ndifference between observed and measured node pressure values,\r\naccounting for the effect of a leak on a set of candidate pipes. The\r\nleak magnitude is the decision variable. This procedure uses an iter-\r\native approach, and the magnitude of the leak is updated at each\r\niteration while minimizing the error in pressure. The mathematical\r\nformulation of the minimization problem is presented in Eq. (20),\r\nfollowed by the definition of each term and the constraint on the\r\ndecision variable. This formulation was adapted from the formu-\r\nlation provided by Berglund et al. (2017) as follows:\r\nmin\r\nx k½Am×n\u0002½xn×1\u0002 −½bn×1\u0002k\r\nAm×n ¼ pleaki −pnoLeaki\r\naj\r\naj ¼ Qj=pα\r\nleaki\r\nb ¼ pobsi −pnoLeaki\r\nsuch that x ≥0\r\nð20Þ\r\nEach element in matrix A is the pressure response coefficient at\r\npressure sensor i ∈f1; : : : ; ng for a leak with coefficient aj simu-\r\nlated at pipe j ∈f1; : : : ; mg. Leak coefficient aj represents the re-\r\nlationship between the volumetric flow rate Q and the pressure p\r\nwith the presence of a leak. aj is initially assumed to be 1.0 to\r\nrepresent 1 m of pressure drop in the presence of a leak when using\r\nSI units and is updated in each step of the optimization. α is the\r\npressure exponent and is assumed to be 0.5 (Crowl and Louvar\r\n2001); pleaki is the simulated pressure in the presence of a leak\r\nretrieved from sensor i; pnoLeaki is the simulated pressure without\r\na …758 tokens truncated…, and the results are\r\nused to constrain the solution space for subsequent optimization\r\nsteps, which apply the iterative LP and iterative MILP procedures.\r\nThe output of the LL-3 subcomponent is the approximate leakage\r\nmagnitude and the corresponding ID of the pipe that most likely\r\ncontains the leak. When more than one magnitude is reported as\r\ngreater than zero, the process selects the pipe with the highest im-\r\npact on the MAS based on the pressure difference analysis (LL-2).\r\nThe presence of more than one solution may be caused by other\r\nexisting leaks, error in the model, or noise in pressure data.\r\nExperimental Settings\r\nCase Study\r\nIn this work, we apply LILA to the L-Town network and the\r\ndata set provided by the organizing committee of BattLeDIM\r\n(Vrachimis et al. 2020a), a worldwide competition carried out in\r\n2020 to assess and compare the results of various leak identification\r\nand localization methods submitted by participants. The L-Town\r\nnetwork (Fig. 2) is a WDN based on an actual network located\r\nin Cyprus. It consists of 785 nodes, 905 pipes with a total length\r\nof 42 km, 1 pump, and 3 valves. The L-Town WDN is divided into\r\nthree district metered areas (DMAs). DMAs A and B are directly\r\nconnected to two water sources, i.e., reservoirs, whereas DMA C is\r\nconnected to a tank that is filled up during night hours by a pump.\r\nL-Town is monitored through SCADA equipment, including flow\r\nrate meters at the exit of each reservoir, water level sensor of the\r\ntank, a set of 33 pressure sensors across the network, and a set of 82\r\nAMR devices connected at the user level within DMA C.\r\nData from pressure sensors, AMR nodes, and flow meters lo-\r\ncated at the reservoirs and pump are reported by SCADA for\r\n2018 and 2019 with a 5-min temporal resolution. SCADA data\r\nwere simulated by the BattleDIM organizers using the WNTR hy-\r\ndraulic simulator (Klise et al. 2018). Moreover, noise was imposed\r\non these data sets by modifying the pipe roughness and diameters to\r\nemulate differences between the hydraulic model and measured\r\ndata and by adding white noise representing measurement noise.\r\nThe 2018 data were released prior to the competition and in-\r\ncluded pressure data from the 33 sensors, demand data from the\r\n82 AMR meters, the water level of the tank, and the flow rate from\r\nreservoirs and the pump. Ground truth information for 10 leakages\r\nreported in 2018 was available and included the time when each\r\nleak was fixed and the corresponding pipe ID. The 2019 data set\r\nincludes time series of the same variables observed in 2018, but no\r\nground truth data were released prior to the BattLeDIM. Hence,\r\nhere we consider ground truth information for the 2019 data set,\r\nwhich was released a posteriori, only as validation data to assess\r\nthe performance of LILA. Over the 2-year period, 33 leaks were\r\nreported in total in L-Town. Besides the 10 reported leakages, 4\r\nadditional leaks occurred in 2018, yet they were not reported as\r\npart of the ground truth training data. These four leakages remained\r\nunfixed and, thus, continued in 2019. An additional 19 leakages\r\nstarted in 2019, summing up to a total of 23 leaks that were relevant\r\nfor the evaluation of the BattLeDIM competition. The leaks are of\r\ntwo types, abrupt and incipient. Abrupt leaks are modeled as pipe\r\nbursts that occur suddenly, reaching their maximum magnitude in-\r\nstantly. Conversely, the magnitude of incipient leaks evolves gradu-\r\nally over time. A calibrated hydraulic model of the network for a\r\nweek of simulation with 5-min hydraulic time steps was also pro-\r\nvided. However, the model and the data have some discrepancies,\r\nas mentioned previously, owing to unknown conditions of the water\r\nsystem, including the actual status of pipes, roughness coefficients,\r\nand specific noise added to the data set by the BattLeDIM organ-\r\nizers [for more details, see Vrachimis and Eliades (2020)].\r\nPerformance Metrics\r\nLeakage detection problems may be regarded as binary classifica-\r\ntion problems where the state with no leak and with leaks, respec-\r\ntively, represent the two classes of interest [cf. e.g., Taormina and\r\nGalelli (2018)]. The accuracy of a binary classifier used for\r\nanomaly detection can be assessed via the following four metrics:\r\ntrue positive (TP) and false positive (FP) indicating correct and in-\r\ncorrect classification of an anomalous state, respectively, and false\r\nnegative (FN) and true negative (TN) indicating incorrect and cor-\r\nrect classification of a normal state, respectively (Lever et al. 2016).\r\nYet in the case of leakage detection, it is only partially of interest\r\nwhether an individual data instance in time (e.g., the nodal pressure\r\nin a single time step) is classified as normal or anomalous, but\r\nrather the onset of the event that causes all subsequent anomalous\r\nstates and its location constitute essential knowledge for the effec-\r\ntive deployment of maintenance staff. To account for this, we assess\r\nLILA with an adapted version of the aforementioned metrics, as\r\nformulated by the BattLeDIM Committee (Vrachimis and Eliades\r\n2020). The adopted definitions of TP, FP, FN, and TN report on the\r\ncorrect labeling of entire leaks regarding spatial and temporal oc-\r\ncurrence rather than single instances in time. As such, TP and FN\r\nhere denote the number of correct and missed leaks, respectively.\r\nGiven the TTD of a leak, defined as the difference between its\r\ndetection time td and its start time tstart\r\nFig. 2. Overview of L-town water distribution network, with high-\r\nlighted pressure sensor locations.\r\n© ASCE\r\n04022025-6\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022025\r\nDownloaded from ascelibrary.org by Andrea Cominola on 03/30/22. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 7\r\nTTD ¼ td −tstart\r\nð21Þ\r\na reported leak is here considered TP if its estimated start time falls\r\nwithin its actual start and end times, tstart and tend (i.e., the maxi-\r\nmum allowed time to detection, TTDmax, is positive and lower than\r\nthe duration of the leak), and it is located within a maximum al-\r\nlowed pipe distance umax from its true leak location. FN refers\r\nto a leak that is not detected at all by the method. FP is defined\r\nas a reported leak that is neither identified in time within the true\r\nleak lifespan nor localized within a distance umax from its actual\r\nlocation. TN is set to zero since it is not applicable in the adapted\r\nformulation of the classification metrics. Because the threshold val-\r\nues TTDmax and umax can be arbitrary, here we also analyze the\r\nsensitivity of the classification performance to varying threshold\r\nvalues.\r\nBesides analyzing individual TP, FP, and FN metrics, we\r\nalso compute the state-of-the-art classification metrics of Recall,\r\nPrecision, and F1-score. Recall measures the ability of a model\r\nto correctly identify anomalous instances in a data set. Precision\r\nmeasures the proportion of correctly identified anomalous instan-\r\nces compared to false alarms. Finally, the F1-score is formulated as\r\nthe weighted harmonic mean of Recall and Precision and varies\r\nbetween zero (worst performance) and one (best performance)\r\n(Lever et al. 2016)\r\nRecall ¼\r\nTP\r\nTP þ FN\r\nð22Þ\r\nPrecision ¼\r\nTP\r\nTP þ FP\r\nð23Þ\r\nF1 ¼ 2 × Recall × Precision\r\nRecall þ Precision\r\nð24Þ\r\nIn addition to the aforementioned classification metrics, we\r\nassess LILA by computing, for each leak, its TTD [Eq. (21) and\r\nTaormina et al. (2018)] and the volume of water leaked Vleaked from\r\nthe start time of the leak tstart until the time of detection td\r\nVleaked ¼\r\nZ td\r\ntstart\r\nQðτÞdτ\r\nð25Þ\r\nFinally, we compute the aggregate economic score S defined by\r\nthe BattLeDIM organizers as\r\nS ¼\r\nX\r\ni\r\npiw þ cir\r\n¼\r\nX\r\ni\r\n\u0004\u0002Z tj\r\nend\r\nti\r\nd\r\nQjðτÞdτ\r\n\u0003\r\n× cw −min\r\n\u0002 uij\r\numax\r\n; 1\r\n\u0003\r\ncR\r\n\u0005\r\nð26Þ\r\nto account for the trade-off between the benefits piw (i.e., NRW re-\r\nduction) a water utility can obtain by the detection i of leakage j\r\nand the costs cir for sending out a repair crew when an alarm is\r\nraised. The benefits piw represent the opportunity cost for water\r\nsaved from td to tend considering leak flow Qj and the cost for water\r\ncw, which is set at €0.80=m3. The repair cost cir relates to the dis-\r\ntance uij between the location of detection i and the actual position\r\nof leak j at a maximum fee cR of €500 (Vrachimis and Eliades\r\n2020).\r\nResults\r\nIn this section, we present the numerical results obtained for leak-\r\nage identification and localization by LILA on the L-Town data set\r\nintroduced in the previous section. We compare the results obtained\r\nby LILA with the benchmark results obtained using an earlier\r\nversion of the algorithm (hereafter called BattLeDIM benchmark),\r\nwhich we submitted for comparative assessment to the BattLeDIM\r\ncompetition (Daniel et al. 2020). LILA enhances the BattLeDIM\r\nbenchmark with regard to both the identification and the localiza-\r\ntion modules. In the leakage identification step, the BattLeDIM\r\nbenchmark does not include an automated change point detection\r\nstep and relies on an expert-based annotation of the leak start time.\r\nLILA’s identification step adds the automatic Cusum method. Fur-\r\nthermore, three improvements were incorporated in the leak locali-\r\nzation step. First, the full set of pressure signals was considered for\r\nSLA optimization in the BattLeDIM benchmark, which added\r\nnoise to the pressure difference. In LILA, only a limited set of sen-\r\nsors around the MAS are considered for each leak analysis. Second,\r\nto avoid the computational burden of analyzing dozens of candidate\r\npipes, the BattLeDIM benchmark picked up to five candidate pipes\r\nspatially distributed around the MAS. Conversely, LILA uses up to\r\n30 candidate pipes from the PDM for each leak event. This led to\r\nalgorithm performance enhancement but also increased substan-\r\ntially the computational time. Third, while the BattLeDIM bench-\r\nmark uses predefined sampling times for SLA, LILA searches for\r\noptimized sampling times for which the differences of observed\r\ndata and simulated data including size-one leaks are minimized\r\nto account for time-variant noise. The demand-calibrated hydraulic\r\nmodel was the same used in the BattLeDIM and LILA versions.\r\nIn the following sections, we first present the overall score and\r\nperformance assessment of LILA and compare it with the BattLe-\r\nDIM benchmark. We then analyze in detail the results obtained for\r\nthe leakage identification and leakage localization steps.\r\nOverall LILA Performance Assessment\r\nThe performance metric results obtained for LILA and the\r\nBattLeDIM benchmark considering the threshold values TTDmax ¼\r\n∞(indicating a maximum allowed TTD equal to the leak duration)\r\nand umax ¼ 300 m defined in the BattLeDIM (Vrachimis and\r\nEliades 2020) are reported in Table 1. LILA correctly identified\r\nand located 17 of the 19 leaks that started in 2019. All leaks were\r\nactually correctly identified, but two leaks were located outside of\r\nthe search radius umax. This good performance is reflected in a\r\nRecall value of 1.0 and slightly lower Precision and F1-score due to\r\nthe inaccurate detection of two leaks. In terms of aggregate score S,\r\nLILA obtained a score of €307,852, outperforming the BattLeDIM\r\nbenchmark, which obtained a total score of €191,055. All obtained\r\nclassification metrics of LILA show a substantial improvement\r\ncompared to the ones reported by the BattLeDIM benchmark. Note\r\nthat both the BattLeDIM benchmark solution and the solution\r\nTable 1. Performance metrics for LILA and BattLeDIM benchmark. The performance metrics are calculated using threshold values TTDmax equal to leak\r\nduration and umax ¼ 300 m consistently with the settings of the BattLeDIM competition\r\nAlgorithm version\r\nTP\r\nFP\r\nFN\r\nRecall\r\nPrecision\r\nF1-score\r\nS (€)\r\nLILA\r\n17\r\n2\r\n0\r\n1.000\r\n0.895\r\n0.944\r\n307,852\r\nBattLeDIM benchmark\r\n10\r\n8\r\n1\r\n0.909\r\n0.556\r\n0.690\r\n191,055\r\n© ASCE\r\n04022025-7\r\nJ. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 8\r\nobtained with LILA did not consider the four leaks that remained\r\nunfixed in 2018 by the L-Town utility and kept running during\r\n2019 as only the 2019 data set was considered for algorithm testing\r\nin this work.\r\nSensitivity of LILA Performance to Classification\r\nThreshold Parameters\r\nIn addition to the foregoing performance assessment using default\r\nthreshold values TTDmax ¼ ∞and umax ¼ 300 m, we evaluate the\r\nsensitivity of the F1-score in response to changes in these two\r\nthreshold parameters. Results are reported in Fig. 3.\r\nThe steeper incline of the curves reaching their maximum at\r\nlower umax indicates that LILA performs better with respect to\r\nthe distance between the estimated and actual leak location. This\r\nis reflected in the lower number of FPs for LILA as more detections\r\nfall within the respective permissible interval of [0; umax]. However,\r\nlower values of the F1-score can be observed for a maximum per-\r\nmissible TTDmax of 1 h. This can be attributed to the difference in\r\nexpert annotation of change points for the BattLeDIM benchmark\r\ncompared to the automated Cusum annotation implemented in\r\nLILA. Most importantly, LILA achieves a perfect F1-score of\r\n1.0 for maximum permissible values of TTDmax ¼ 41 days and\r\numax ¼ 374 m. For these or any greater values of the two upper\r\nthreshold parameters, LILA is capable of correctly identifying\r\nand localizing all leaks in the data set.\r\nDetailed Assessment of Leakage Identification\r\nFig. 4 shows four exemplary leakage situations that occurred in the\r\nL-Town network across different time periods during 2019. For\r\neach leak, the signal of the ground truth leak flow Qleak is compared\r\nto the MRE from the corresponding MAS. From this representation\r\nit is apparent that occurring leaks are directly reflected in the MRE\r\nsince MRE rises with leak occurrence and diminishes again with\r\nleak repair. For instance, the start of the abrupt Leak 1 at Pipe p523\r\nin Fig. 4(a) is not preceded by other leaks, resulting in low MRE\r\nvalues before and high values after its occurrence. With the onset of\r\nLeak 2 at Pipe p827, both MRE signals of n506 (closest to Leak 1)\r\nand n726 (closest to Leak 2) show an increase in magnitude com-\r\npared to the situation with no leaks present, indicating that LILA is\r\ncapable of detecting overlapping leaks. Incipient leaks are repre-\r\nsented in the MRE in the same fashion, even reflecting the growth\r\nof the leak, as can be observed in Fig. 4(b). However, with the onset\r\nof abrupt Leak 6 at Pipe p514 during the growth process of Leak 4\r\nat Pipe p653, the latter is temporarily obscured in this representa-\r\ntion and only becomes visible again at a later stage. Similarly, the\r\ninitial appearance of Leak 10 at Pipe p193 in Fig. 4(c) is obscured\r\nby the presence of both Leaks 7 and 8 at Pipes p331 and p142,\r\nrespectively. For this and other similar instances, we analyzed indi-\r\nvidual trajectories of MREji [cf. Eq. (13)] to obtain more precise\r\nstart times. The complete set of all individual trajectories that were\r\nused for the subsequent change point detection is displayed in Fig. 5\r\nand listed in Table 2.\r\nA special case, represented in Fig. 4(d), arises in DMA C, where\r\nunknown irregular demand patterns are the cause of large noise in\r\nthe MRE, obscuring individual leaks and making their identifica-\r\ntion more difficult. Hence, we chose to display the daily rolling\r\nmean instead of the raw MRE signal for better inspection. None-\r\ntheless, LILA is able to identify both leaks occurring in DMA C by\r\nanalyzing the raw MRE signal.\r\nMoreover, Fig. 6 shows two exemplary MRE trajectories that\r\nwere analyzed for change point detection (LI-3): on the left the\r\nabrupt Leak 1, on the right the incipient Leak 12. For both cases\r\nthe signal-to-noise ratio enables a clear visual identification of the\r\nleak’s development. Looking at the daily rolling means facilitates\r\nthe visual inspection. It is worth noting that in the presence of either\r\nexemplary leak, further leaks started in different parts of the same\r\nDMA. Yet the trajectories enable making a clear distinction between\r\noverlapping leaks and are therefore well suited for the application of\r\ndifferent change point detection methods. For a more detailed dis-\r\ncussion on the effects of simultaneous leaks, we refer the reader\r\nto the “Discussion” section.\r\nFig. 7 compares the results of expert and Cusum change\r\npoint annotation for abrupt [Figs. 7(a and c)] and incipient\r\n[Figs. 7(b and d)] leaks in terms of both TTD [Figs. 7(a and b)]\r\nand Vleaked [Figs. 7(c and d)]. The observation from Fig. 6 as to\r\nwhich abrupt leaks are detected much faster and more coherently\r\nthan incipient leaks generalizes for all leaks and detection methods.\r\nThe experts annotated most abrupt leaks on the spot with a maxi-\r\nmum delay of 35 min (cf. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022025\r\nDownloaded from ascelibrary.org by Andrea Cominola on 03/30/22. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 11\r\nIn this work, we present the capability of the enhanced\r\nidentification-localization method LILA, which includes the\r\nanalysis of all top 30 pipes from the PDM as candidates for each\r\nevent in the localization step. Furthermore, for each leak, we\r\nonly used the MAS combined with three to five additional sen-\r\nsors to avoid extra noise that might be introduced by sensors not\r\naffected by some spatially distant leaks. Each additional sensor\r\nwas selected for each event based on signal strength. Depending\r\nonly on the signal and not on topological or geographical con-\r\nditions, some leaks were analyzed with three and others with up\r\nto five sensors in addition to the MAS. Fig. 8 displays the effect\r\nof this adaption by comparing the BattLeDIM benchmark and\r\nthe LILA algorithms.\r\nLILA finds leaks with an average search radius of 149 m. Of\r\nthe 19 leaks localized during 2019, 37% was within a 100-m\r\nradius, 53% of leaks had a distance ranging from 100 and 300 m\r\nfrom the true leaking pipe, and 10% were located outside of the\r\nmaximum allowable distance of 300 m used in the BattLeDIM\r\ncompetition. There are two events where the localization step\r\nfound the exact location, Leaks 8 and 19, whereas Leak 9 was\r\nthe event reported by LILA with the maximum distance from\r\nthe true location (374 m) (Fig. 9). The localization step does\r\nnot differentiate between incipient and abrupt leaks; however,\r\nowing to the multiple sample times to retrieve pressure values,\r\nSLA reports slightly better performance for the incipient leaks\r\ncompared to abrupt leaks. While LILA outperforms the BattLe-\r\nDIM benchmark, there are events where the localization step does\r\nnot improve the location reported by the BattLeDIM benchmark\r\nbecause of the noise in the data and the difference between the\r\ndetected and the true start time of the leaks. SLA depends on a\r\nwell-calibrated pressure model reporting values close enough\r\nto the values reported by SCADA. When there is substantial error\r\nbetween the measured and simulated pressures, the performance\r\nof the SLA approach is significantly affected.\r\n(a)\r\n(b)\r\nFig. 6. Exemplary MRE trajectories during leakages and their respective annotations by expert and Cusum method. (a) Leak 1 (abrupt); and (b) Leak\r\n12 (incipient).\r\n(a)\r\n(b)\r\n(c)\r\n(d)\r\nFig. 7. Comparison of expert and Cusum leak annotation: (a, b) time to detection; and (c, d) leaked water volume. Results are reported for abrupt (a, c)\r\nand incipient (b, d) leaks. No bars indicate no detection delay or no water loss. The y-axis range in (a) is limited to [0,25] hours for visualization\r\npurposes, but the leak located in pipe p280 was detected by LILA with a delay of 116 h and 10 min (see Table 2).\r\n© ASCE\r\n04022025-11\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022025\r\nDownloaded from ascelibrary.org by Andrea Cominola on 03/30/22. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 12\r\nDiscussion\r\nThe numerical results we obtained by testing LILA on data from\r\nthe L-Town WDN case study overall demonstrated its suitability\r\nto perform leakage identification and localization based on pres-\r\nsure data with high temporal resolution. Almost all ground truth\r\nleakages were promptly identified and were localized within an\r\nacceptable distance from the actual location. Yet the following\r\nspecific methodological aspects of the leakage identification and\r\nleakage localization phases in LILA require further investigation\r\nto make it of general value and ultimately usable for complex\r\nreal-world applications.\r\nFirst, as mentioned in the “Methods” section, the current version\r\nof the proposed LILA algorithm is not able to automatically iden-\r\ntify leaks that occur in DMAs where unknown, irregular demand\r\npatterns are present (i.e., as in DMA C of the L-Town WDN). When\r\nirregular demand patterns are known, e.g., pump operation in the\r\nL-Town WDN, they may be included in the regression model used\r\nfor leakage identification and, hence, pose no problem to the method.\r\nYet, unknown irregular demand patterns cannot be easily included in\r\nthe model, leading to higher residual error of the regression model\r\nand hindering the analysis of leakages. If LILA were coupled with\r\na method capable of learning unknown demand patterns from the\r\navailable data, it would become suitable for analysis on any WDN,\r\nregardless of the characteristics of its demand patterns.\r\nAnother important factor in the successful application of the\r\nidentification phase of LILA is the availability of a time period\r\nin the absence of newly occurring leaks or other similar transient\r\neffects for training or calibration purposes. Within this work, we\r\nselected the training period manually based on preliminary data ex-\r\nploration and visualization to obtain low values of the model\r\nresidual for this period. Because the regression model for leakage\r\nidentification [Eq. (12)] may have to be recalibrated after the\r\noccurrence of a new leak or due to changing demand, a manual\r\napproach may be impractical for real-world applications. A more\r\nscalable version of LILAwould require an automatic selection of an\r\noptimal subset of data for training.\r\nTable 3. Performance comparison of localization methodology (BattLeDIM benchmark versus enhanced, selective version LILA) applied to L-Town network\r\nfor leaks of 2019. Distances of identified leaking pipes to true leak location for (a) abrupt; and (b) incipient leaks.\r\n© ASCE\r\n04022025-12\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022025\r\nDownloaded from ascelibrary.org by Andrea Cominola on 03/30/22. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 13\r\nThird, unlike the BattLeDIM benchmark, the leakage identifi-\r\ncation phase in LILA incorporates automated change point detec-\r\ntion. We implemented an online change point detection method\r\n(see “Methods” section) to make LILA more suitable for an appli-\r\ncation within a real-world setting, compared to the expert-based\r\nestimation of leak start times of the BattLeDIM version. However,\r\nthe numerical results we obtained using the online change point\r\ndetection method revealed a significant drawback regarding the\r\nTTD in comparison with the expert-based annotation used in the\r\nBattLeDIM benchmark. It is worth noting that the expert-based an-\r\nnotations were made ex-post, rather corresponding to an offline\r\nfashion of change point detection. This consideration opens up\r\nthe need for a more systematic analysis to compare the performance\r\nof different offline and online change point detection methods for\r\nthe analysis of the residual error produced by the leakage identifi-\r\ncation regression model. While online change point detection may\r\nentail quicker availability of the results, i.e., alarms are raised more\r\npromptly, the application of offline detection methods with a limited\r\ntime horizon may also be accurate and lead to timely identification\r\nof leaks. The choice of an optimal change point detection method\r\nshould therefore not be limited to either online or offline capabil-\r\nities, but it is rather the solution of a multicriteria problem that con-\r\nsiders the trade-offs between TTD, effort needed for calibration,\r\nease of implementation, and low susceptibility to false alarms.\r\nAnother factor that needs further investigation is the method’s\r\nsensitivity to leaks that occur in temporal and spatial proximity.\r\nThe method presented here was able to distinguish between multiple\r\nparallel leaks that occurred in the BattleDIM data sets, but we have no\r\ninformation about the capabilities or limitations of the method in\r\nidentifying parallel leaks for other data sets. Further research can in-\r\nvolve experiments that vary spatial and temporal distances between\r\nleaks to explore the method’s boundaries. We expect that the re-\r\nsults will depend on the availability and placement of sensing equip-\r\nment in the network, which is another interesting subject for further\r\nresearch.\r\nFinally, the SLA used in the leakage localization phase relies on\r\nan almost error-free model, where the only disturbance between the\r\nmeasured and simulated pressure is due to an unusual event.\r\nHowever, an error-free model is rarely available. The error induced\r\nby the actual status of the network components and the noise of\r\nthe measured data prevent SLA from accurately identifying the\r\ntrue leaking pipe. To partially address this issue, we modified the\r\ndemand patterns and the base demand of the hydraulic model to\r\nimprove the match between the simulated and measured data across\r\nthe network. However, the new demand patterns do not distinguish\r\nthe type of user, residential or commercial, and overwrite the original\r\ndemand patterns of the network with the new patterns. Hence, alter-\r\nnative ways to mitigate the effect of model errors need further inves-\r\ntigation. Correction of model error can leverage the combination of\r\nidentification and localization to provide a reliable set of leaks in\r\nresearch studies and practical applications (van Thienen 2021).\r\nConclusions\r\nIn this paper we developed and described LILA, a pressure-driven\r\nalgorithm for leakage identification and localization in WDNs. We\r\ndeveloped LILA within the scope of the BattleDIM (Vrachimis\r\net al. 2020a), where we submitted the results from an early version\r\nof the algorithm (Daniel et al. 2020).\r\nLILA includes a sequential two-step procedure composed of a\r\nleakage identification module and a leakage localization module.\r\nThe leakage identification module detects the presence of a leak,\r\nestimates its starting time, and pinpoints the pressure sensor that is\r\nmost affected by the leak (called MAS in this paper). The leakage\r\nlocalization module uses the information provided by the identifi-\r\ncation step and a calibrated hydraulic model to estimate the location\r\nof the identified leakages. First, a leak is simulated on a set of can-\r\ndidate pipes. Then successive linear approximation is applied to\r\nfind the location and magnitude of the leak by minimizing the dif-\r\nference between measured and simulated pressure values. Com-\r\npared to its initial version introduced in Daniel et al. (2020), we\r\nrefined LILAwith the inclusion of more candidate pipes for leakage\r\nlocalization and by restricting the search using only the MAS for\r\nFig. 9. True and detected leaks that started in 2019 using LILA. Circled numbers represent event number as shown in Table 3.\r\n© ASCE\r\n04022025-13\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022025\r\nDownloaded from ascelibrary.org by Andrea Cominola on 03/30/22. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 14\r\neach event, which improved the computational burden of the\r\nsimulation-optimization method used in leakage localization.\r\nWe tested the ability of LILA to solve the challenge of finding a\r\nset of leaks occurring during 2019 in the L-Town WDN, a virtual\r\nrepresentation of a real water network in Cyprus, created for the\r\nBattLeDIM (Vrachimis et al. 2020a). In light of the numerical re-\r\nsults we obtained by assessing the performance of LILAwith multi-\r\nple performance metrics based on leak classification accuracy,\r\nestimation accuracy of the leak start time and location, and an ag-\r\ngregate monetary score, we assert that LILA can accurately identify\r\nand localize leakages occurring in WDNs monitored with a distrib-\r\nuted network of pressure sensors collecting data with high resolu-\r\ntion (5-min temporal resolution in this work). The initial version\r\nof our algorithm presented as part of the BattLeDIM by our Leak-\r\nbusters team (Daniel et al. 2020) and used in this paper as a bench-\r\nmark achieved a score of €191,055 and was ranked third by the\r\nBattLeDIM organizers, out of 18 participating teams worldwide.\r\nThe improved version presented in this paper with the name “LILA”\r\nachieved a total economic score of €307,852, which outperforms\r\neconomically the score of €260,562 obtained by the winning team\r\nof the BattLeDIM (Vrachimis and Eliades 2020). Finally, this en-\r\nhancement in performance comes at a computational cost. While\r\nthe BattLeDIM version used 5 candidate pipes selected based on\r\ngeographical proximity to the MAS, LILA uses up to 30 candidate\r\npipes selected based on the pressure difference analysis described in\r\nsection LL-2: Pressure Difference Analysis and increased the run-\r\nning time by over 20 times for each leak.\r\nDetailed results showed that LILA identified all 19 considered\r\nleaks, with an overall F1-score exceeding 0.94 considering the\r\nthreshold values of the BattLeDIM and could obtain an F1-score\r\nof 1.0 for maximum permissible distances of umax ¼ 374 m.\r\nAbrupt leakages were identified immediately or with little delay,\r\nusually less than 2 h, while up to 20 days were required on average\r\nby automatic change point detection to raise alarms on incipient\r\nleakages. Finally, LILA could localize leaks within an average\r\ndistance of 149 m from the true leaking pipe. These results are\r\npromising in terms of moving forward with the development of\r\nleakage detection algorithms capable of exploiting the increasing\r\namount of online sensor-based information gathered and transmit-\r\nted by pressure sensors distributed in WDNs.\r\nOverall, the contributions and limitations of this work offer sev-\r\neral opportunities for further research, primarily related to making\r\nLILA of general use and applicability in real-world settings. First,\r\nwe acknowledge that the prompt identification of incipient leaks\r\nremains a challenge for many change point detection methods,\r\nso more comparative analyses and investigations would be needed\r\nto improve its identification performance without resulting in an\r\nincrease in the FP rate. Second, model-based leakage localization\r\nis sensitive to model errors. While LILA was able to localize leak-\r\nages with an average distance of 149 m in the case study presented\r\nin this paper, the requirement of an almost error-free pressure-\r\ncalibrated model would cause a dramatic decrease in localization\r\nperformance with models available in most real-world cases. More-\r\nover, the selection of the candidate pipes can still be, also in the\r\nrefined version of LILA, a computationally expensive procedure\r\nbecause it relies on simulating a leak at every single pipe of the\r\nsystem and measuring its impact on the pressure sensors installed\r\nacross the system. Finally, irregular water demand should be mod-\r\neled and incorporated into LILA and stress tests with real-time\r\nSCADA data and benchmarking in real-world applications would\r\nmake it suitable for use in practice.\r\nMore benchmark data sets and testing platforms similar to the\r\nBattLeDIM and structured collaborative work with water utilities\r\nwilling to leverage their ongoing digitalization efforts with inte-\r\ngrated data-driven analytics would enable further development\r\nand testing of methods like LILA with the ultimate goal of address-\r\ning the aforementioned open challenges and ultimately making\r\nthem suitable and fully reliable for inclusion in utilities’ leakage\r\nmanagement operations.\r\nAppendix. Choice of Hyperparameters for the\r\nCusum Change Point Detection Method\r\nThe Cusum method requires an estimate of the signal standard\r\ndeviation, a choice of δ, and a threshold parameter H ¼ η × σx,\r\nwhich itself is related to the choice of δ [cf. Eq. (16)]. To assess\r\nthe algorithm’s ability to robustly flag the leaks and to select the\r\nhyperparameters in a reasonable range, we first applied the Cusum\r\nmethod in a controlled environment. Since leak size correlates with\r\n(a)\r\n(b)\r\nFig. 10. (a) Exemplary transient leak error trajectory for different noise levels (a leak/noise level of 2 corresponds to Gaussian noise with standard\r\ndeviation of half the maximum leak magnitude); and (b) overview Cusum-hyperparameter search. The heatmap color indicates the average detection\r\ndelay, and numbers represent optimal threshold levels η. Configurations without optimal threshold levels did not produce a result without false\r\npositives and were therefore neglected.\r\n© ASCE\r\n04022025-14\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\r\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022025\r\nDownloaded from ascelibrary.org by Andrea Cominola on 03/30/22. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\r\nPAGE 15\r\npressure drops and therefore with the MRE development, we took\r\nthe leak water loss trajectories (ground truth) and added random\r\nnoise to get a realistic estimate of the MRE structure [Fig. 10(a)].\r\nWe then applied the algorithm for various configurations of δ and η.\r\nThe results are displayed in Fig. 10(b).\r\nIn practice, the MRE standard deviation can be estimated over\r\nthe calibration period with no leak. Moreover, it was empirically\r\nfound that a suitable combination of the hyperparameters for the\r\nCusum method are δ ¼ 4 and η ¼ 3, which is indeed close to the\r\ntheoretical results. However, in DMA C, these settings needed to be\r\nadjusted because of the irregular demand patterns’ impact on the\r\nMRE. There, we used a δ of 1 and η ¼ 300 to account for the strong\r\npatterns in the MRE time series.\r\nData Availability Statement\r\nSome or all data, models, or code generated or used during the\r\nstudy are available in online repositories in accordance with funder\r\ndata retention policies (Vrachimis et al. 2020b; Daniel et al. 2021;\r\nPesantez 2021). \r\ndef leak_analysis(nodes,scada_data,cor_time_frame):\r\n    N = len(nodes)\r\n    T = scada_data.pressures.shape[0]\r\n    P = scada_data.pressures[nodes].values\r\n    V = scada_data.flows['PUMP_1'].values\r\n    \r\n    K0 = np.zeros((N,N))\r\n    K1 = np.zeros((N,N))\r\n    Kd = np.zeros((N,N))\r\n    \r\n    ref_nodes = []\r\n    for i,node in enumerate(nodes):\r\n        ref_node = Ref_node(node)\r\n        models=[]\r\n\r\n        X_tr = np.concatenate([scada_data.pressures[node].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1),\r\n                               scada_data.flows['PUMP_1'].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1)],\r\n                             axis=1)\r\n\r\n        for j,node_cor in enumerate(nodes):\r\n            y_tr = scada_data.pressures[node_cor].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1)\r\n            model = LinearRegression()\r\n            models.append(model.fit(X_tr, y_tr))\r\n            K0[i,j] = model.intercept_[0]\r\n            K1[i,j] = model.coef_[0][0]\r\n            Kd[i,j] = model.coef_[0][1]\r\n        ref_node.set_models(models)\r\n    \r\n    np.fill_diagonal(K0,0)\r\n    np.fill_diagonal(K1,1)\r\n    np.fill_diagonal(Kd,0)\r\n    \r\n    e = np.multiply.outer(K0,np.ones(T))\\\r\n        + np.multiply.outer(Kd,V)\\\r\n        + np.multiply.outer(K1,np.ones(T))*np.multiply.outer(P,np.ones(N)).transpose(1,2,0)\\\r\n        - np.multiply.outer(P,np.ones(N)).transpose(2,1,0)\r\n    \r\n    if 'n215' in nodes:\r\n        e[nodes.index('n215'),:,:] *= 0.02\r\n    \r\n    res = np.zeros((N,T))\r\n    for t in range(T):\r\n        e_t_i = e[:,:,t].copy()\r\n        e_t_i[e_t_i<0]=0\r\n        e_t_i[e_t_i>0]=1\r\n        i_ = pd.Series(e_t_i.sum(axis=0)).sort_values(ascending=False).index.to_list()\r\n        i_max = int(i_[0])\r\n        \r\n        res[i_max,t] = np.linalg.norm(e[:,:,t][:,i_max])\r\n        \r\n    MRE = pd.DataFrame(res.T,index=scada_data.pressures.index,columns=nodes)\r\n    return MRE\r\n    \r\ndef leak_analysis_individual(scada_data,node_MAS,closest_nodes,time_frame_list):\r\n    states = []\r\n    for time_frame in time_frame_list:\r\n        states.append(State(start=time_frame[0],end=time_frame[3],cor_start=time_frame[1],cor_end=time_frame[2]))\r\n\r\n    for state in states:\r\n        models=[]\r\n        cor_time_frame = [state._cor_start,state._cor_end]\r\n\r\n        X_tr = np.concatenate([scada_data.pressures[node_MAS].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1),\r\n                               scada_data.flows['PUMP_1'].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1)],\r\n                             axis=1)\r\n\r\n        for node_cor in scada_data.pressures.columns.to_numpy():\r\n            y_tr = scada_data.pressures[node_cor].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1)\r\n            model = LinearRegression()\r\n            models.append(model.fit(X_tr, y_tr))\r\n        state.set_models(models)\r\n\r\n    df_state = pd.DataFrame(np.zeros((scada_data.pressures.shape[0],scada_data.pressures.shape[1])),\r\n                            columns=scada_data.pressures.columns,\r\n                            index=scada_data.pressures.index)\r\n\r\n    for i_ in range(len(states)):\r\n        models = states[i_]._models_Reg\r\n        start = states[i_]._start\r\n        end = states[i_]._end\r\n\r\n        for node_cor in closest_nodes:\r\n            df_error = pd.DataFrame(index=scada_data.pressures.index)\r\n\r\n            y_test = scada_data.pressures[node_cor].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1)\r\n            X_test = np.concatenate([scada_data.pressures[node_MAS].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1),\r\n                                     scada_data.flows['PUMP_1'].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1)],\r\n                                   axis=1)\r\n\r\n            df_error['e'] = y_test - models[scada_data.pressures.columns.tolist().index(node_cor)].predict(X_test)\r\n            df_state[node_cor].loc[start:end] = df_error['e'].loc[start:end]\r\n\r\n    \r\n\r\n    return df_state[closest_nodes]\r\n\r\ndef plot_MRE(MRE,ground_truth=None,leak_id=None,ind=False,roll=None,ToD=None,zoom_factor=1):\r\n    if not ind:\r\n        plt.style.use(['default'])\r\n        f,ax = plt.subplots(1,sharex=True,sharey=True,figsize=(18,7))\r\n        locator = mdates.AutoDateLocator(minticks=7, maxticks=15)\r\n        formatter = mdates.ConciseDateFormatter(locator)\r\n        formatter.offset_formats[2] = '%Y'\r\n\r\n        for i,node in enumerate(MRE.columns):\r\n            if roll is None:\r\n                ax.plot(MRE[node],label=node,color=plt.get_cmap('gist_ncar')(i/len(MRE.columns)))\r\n            else:\r\n                ax.plot(MRE[node].rolling(roll).mean(),label=node,color=plt.get_cmap('gist_ncar')(i/len(MRE.columns)))\r\n\r\n        ax.set_ylabel('$\\Vert MRE_{s}(t) \\Vert_F$',fontsize=24)\r\n        ax.legend(prop={'size': 24},loc=6,ncol=2,bbox_to_anchor=(1.02, 0.5))\r\n        for tick in ax.xaxis.get_major_ticks():\r\n            tick.label.set_fontsize(24)\r\n        for tick in ax.yaxis.get_major_ticks():\r\n            tick.label.set_fontsize(24) \r\n        f.tight_layout()\r\n        plt.show()\r\n    else:\r\n        plt.style.use(['default'])\r\n        f,ax = plt.subplots(1,sharex=True,figsize=(9,4))\r\n        locator = mdates.AutoDateLocator(minticks=7, maxticks=15)\r\n        formatter = mdates.ConciseDateFormatter(locator)\r\n        formatter.offset_formats[2] = '%Y'\r\n        ax.get_xaxis().set_major_locator(locator)\r\n"
  }
]

The NMF is considered stable for a certain part of network, so changes of NMF\r\nindicates occurrences of potential leakage in this DMA. Concerned workers could then isolate the\r\nexact leaking location by acoustic equipment or get precise geographic information from acoustic\r\nmonitors installed in network. Data-driven models for leakage detection have been proposed since then. Mounce et al\r\n(2010) proposed an artificial neural network combined with a fuzzy inference system to detect pipe\r\nbursts. Ye & Fenner (2011) proposed an adaptive Kalman filtering method which is more efficient.\r\nOther methods such as Bayesian inference system and Golden Section method are also proposed by\r\nresearchers and engineers. Such seasonality factors will be\r\nconsidered respectively for simulation of different months.\r\nTable 1 Demand Seasonality of Each Category\r\n\r\nWhile demand patterns and seasonality can be determined by analysis of AMR demand data, some\r\nmodel parameters remain uncertain and hard to analyze, such as pipe roughness, effective diameter\r\nand nodal base demand. As suggested by the utility company of “L-Town”, all these parameters could\r\nhave no more than 10% difference of the nominal values. Genetic algorithm is used to optimize this\r\nproblem.\r\nVariables: 𝐶𝑑𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑛𝑑,𝑟 , 𝐶𝑑𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑛𝑑,𝑐, 𝐶𝑟𝑜𝑢𝑔ℎ𝑛𝑒𝑠𝑠, 𝐶𝐷\r\nConstraints: 𝐶𝑑𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑛𝑑,𝑟 , 𝐶𝑑𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑛𝑑,𝑐, 𝐶𝑟𝑜𝑢𝑔ℎ𝑛𝑒𝑠𝑠 𝑖𝑛 [0.9, 1.1], 𝐶𝐷 𝑖𝑛 [0.9, 1]\r\nObjective Function: 𝑚𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑚𝑖𝑧𝑒 𝐿𝑜𝑠𝑠= 𝑚𝑖𝑛\r\n∑\r\n∑\r\n(𝑃𝑡,𝑖−𝑃𝑡,𝑖,0)2\r\n𝑁\r\n𝑛=1\r\n𝑇\r\n𝑡=1\r\n𝑁∙𝑇\r\n\r\nWhere 𝐶𝑑𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑛𝑑,𝑟 and 𝐶𝑑𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑛𝑑,𝑐 represent demand variation coefficients of residential and\r\ncommercial users; 𝐶𝑟𝑜𝑢𝑔ℎ𝑛𝑒𝑠𝑠 and 𝐶𝐷 represents variation coefficients of pipe roughness and\r\neffective diameter. Machine learning classification models are constructed to learn pressure change\r\ncharacteristics of each area, and the leakage category is identified from the pressure situation at each\r\nmoment by the model. LightGBM and LSTM are selected to achieve the classification goal.\r\nIn order to build the training dataset of the classification model, labels with leakage area’s sensor ID\r\n(The column number of each monitoring sensor in the table) or no leakage (marked as 0) are marked\r\nat each time step. The calibrated hydraulic model is used to simulate numerous leakage events with\r\nWNTR, ensuring every leakage condition is recorded. The simulated results and 2018 measured\r\nSCADA data are then combined as the training and testing dataset. Firstly, the difference between\r\nthe pressure value P and the referential value of the current monitoring point is extracted as ΔPt = Pt\r\n- [0.95*(Pmax - Pmin) + Pmin)]. Then, a 1-hour average pressure deficit at each moment of each sensor is\r\nextracted as the pressure feature. The sample balance of the dataset is then adjusted to ensure all types\r\nof leakage situations are learned equally by the model. At last, the dataset is scaled to a certain range\r\nwith the same scale to ensure that various types of feature values have the same weight, the\r\nMinMaxScaler is used here as a scaler.\r\n1.3 Leakage Isolation\r\nGiven the predicted results from data classification models, a genetic algorithm-based leakage\r\nisolation model is applied. Leakage events are simulated iteratively in EPANET and pressure results\r\nare then compared with the measured data, the most matched simulation indicates the possible leakage\r\nevent in reality. The optimization problem is described as follows.\r\nVariables: 𝐿𝑁𝑖 , 𝐾𝑖\r\nConstraints: 𝐾𝑖 𝑖𝑛 [0, 𝐾max], 𝑃𝑖\r\n> 0, 𝑁𝐿𝑑𝑢𝑝= 0\r\nWhere 𝐿𝑁𝑖 is the index for leakage node i; 𝐾𝑖 is the emitter coefficient for leakage node i, which\r\nis used to simulate leakages in EPANET; 𝐾𝑚𝑎𝑥 is the maximum emitter coefficient; 𝑃𝑖 is the\r\npressure at leakage node i; 𝑁𝐿𝑑𝑢𝑝 is the number of the duplicated nodes identified as leakages by\r\none simulation,\r\nObjective Function: 𝑚𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑚𝑖𝑧𝑒 𝐹(𝐿𝑁𝑖 , 𝐾𝑖) = 𝑚𝑖𝑛 ∑\r\n∑\r\n[\r\n𝐻𝑛(𝑡)\r\n𝑆\r\n−𝐻𝑛(𝑡)\r\n𝑂\r\n𝐻𝑛(𝑡)\r\n𝑂\r\n]2\r\n𝑁\r\n𝑛=1\r\n𝑁\r\n𝑇\r\n𝑡=1\r\n\r\nwhere 𝐻𝑛(𝑡)\r\n𝑂\r\nand 𝐻𝑛(𝑡)\r\n𝑆\r\nare the observed head and simulated head at junction n at time step t; N is\r\nthe number of observed head.\r\nA potential leakage event is represented as the index of leakage node with a positive emitter\r\ncoefficient. Binary code is used for encoding the two variables that the node ID is designated as a\r\nPAGE 4\r\n4\r\n\r\nnode index and the emitter coefficient is encoded with a value between zero and the prescribed\r\nmaximum value with a specified increment. By applying such scheme, the maximum number of\r\nleakage nodes is specified and does not have to be the same as the total number of nodes. The\r\noptimized number of leakage nodes, where the calculated emitter coefficients are greater than zero,\r\nshould be less than the specified maximum number of leakage nodes to be detected. Otherwise, and\r\nthe model should be re-run for leakage detection.\r\n2. Results and Discussions\r\nSince the data volume of hydraulic simulations is much larger than the real data volume in 2018, 20%\r\nof the 2018 data is used as the validation set, the remaining part and hydraulic simulation data are\r\ncombined as the training set, ensuring that the model can learn the measured data better. The two\r\nmodels are trained separately, and the model parameters and model structure are adjusted and\r\noptimized according to the test results. The training and testing loss of these two models are shown\r\nin Figure 3. The accuracies of LSTM and LightGBM are 95.85% and 97.27%, respectively. The\r\npredicted leakage time periods and leakage area’s sensor ID of possible leakage events in 2018 are\r\nlist in Table 3, these results are used for leakage isolation algorithm.\r\n\r\nFigure 3 Model loss results of LSTM (left) and LightGBM (right)\r\nTable 3 2018 Real Leakage Repair Time VS.
